## Document Contract

### 0.1 Identity

This document is the Universal Governance document. Its persistent Document ID is `DIUA-DIC-000003`, allocated through the applicable UNIS/UNIR identification and allocation mechanism. The filename and repository path shall not substitute for Document Identity.

### 0.2 Responsibility Boundary

Universal Governance is responsible for Universal-level governance authority, decision mechanisms, controlled evolution, conformance, accountability, and governance integrity within its defined scope.

### 0.3 Scope Boundary

This document governs Universal-level governance concerns. It does not replace Universal Architecture, foundational Universal Naming & Identification authority, Universal Standard semantics, Registry State authority, or domain-specific governance within their reserved scopes.

### 0.4 Canonical Interpretation Rule

This document is canonically locked as Version 1.0. Any explicitly deferred or open matter remains governed by its stated scope and shall not be inferred as additional normative authority.

### 0.5 Materialization Rule

This Markdown file is the controlled canonical materialization of the Universal Governance document. Its canonical status is established by the applicable governance authorization and canonical lock records. Materialization does not independently create or alter authority, canonicality, or governance scope.

### 0.6 Navigation Rule

The Markdown heading hierarchy and the navigation index below are the primary navigation mechanisms for human and AI consumers.

### 0.7 AI Authority Boundary

AI may retrieve, interpret, summarize, cross-reference, transform, generate, and perform authorized validation of this document, but shall not infer canonical authority from filename, repository position, recency, search ranking, or AI generation status.

## 0. Document Foundation

### 0.1 Purpose

Universal Governance defines the governance framework through which the Universal system is directed, controlled, maintained, evolved, and kept conformant with its established architectural foundation.

Universal Governance exists as a distinct canonical document from Universal Architecture.

Universal Architecture establishes the structural foundation of the Universal system. Universal Governance establishes the authority, rules, decision mechanisms, controls, responsibilities, lifecycle mechanisms, and change processes through which that architecture and the systems built upon it are governed.

Universal Governance shall therefore govern the **operation and evolution of the Universal system** without redefining the architectural model established by Universal Architecture.

### 0.2 Foundational Relationship

The relationship between the two documents is:

**Universal Architecture → defines the structural foundation.**

**Universal Governance → governs the authority, control, evolution, and conformance of that foundation and the Universal system operating upon it.**

Universal Governance shall not absorb architectural definitions merely because those definitions are relevant to governance.

Likewise, Universal Architecture shall not be expanded to contain governance mechanisms merely because those mechanisms operate upon architectural structures.

### 0.3 Governance Objective

Universal Governance shall ensure that the Universal system:

1. remains internally coherent;
2. preserves its canonical foundations;
3. has explicit authority for decisions and changes;
4. maintains controlled evolution;
5. prevents unauthorized semantic or structural drift;
6. establishes clear ownership and decision rights;
7. provides mechanisms for conformance and compliance;
8. preserves historical and decision traceability;
9. protects boundaries between Universal-level and domain-level authority; and
10. provides a stable governance foundation for all subordinate Universal systems, standards, registries, architectures, and production frameworks.

### 0.4 Governance Scope

Universal Governance governs Universal-level concerns including:

- governance authority;
- governance principles;
- decision rights;
- authority hierarchy;
- responsibility and accountability;
- canonicality;
- change governance;
- amendment and revision control;
- lifecycle governance;
- conformance governance;
- exception governance;
- conflict resolution;
- dependency and boundary governance;
- registry authority;
- canonical publication;
- supersession and retirement;
- governance evidence and traceability;
- architectural evolution governance; and
- interaction between Universal-level governance and subordinate domain governance.

Universal Governance does **not** replace the governance mechanisms of individual domains.

Domain-specific governance may exist beneath the Universal Governance framework, provided that it does not contradict or override applicable Universal-level authority.

---

## 1. Governance Principles

### 1.1 Architectural Primacy

Universal Architecture is the established structural foundation of the Universal system.

Governance decisions shall operate against that foundation and shall not silently redefine it.

Any governance decision that requires an architectural change shall identify that architectural dependency explicitly and follow the applicable architectural change process.

### 1.2 Separation of Architecture and Governance

Architecture and governance are distinct normative concerns.

Architecture answers:

> **What is the Universal system and how is it structurally organized?**

Governance answers:

> **Who has authority over it, according to which rules, through which decisions, and under what controls?**

Neither layer shall be used as an implicit substitute for the other.

### 1.3 Explicit Authority

Every normative Universal decision shall have an identifiable source of authority.

No document, registry, standard, process, or operational practice shall become Universal-level authority merely through repeated use or informal acceptance.

Authority shall be established through an explicit governance mechanism.

### 1.4 Canonicality by Authorization

A document or artifact becomes canonical through an explicit authorization process.

Existence, publication, repository presence, implementation, or historical usage alone does not establish canonical authority.

### 1.5 Controlled Evolution

The Universal system shall be capable of evolving without losing structural continuity.

Changes shall therefore be evaluated for:

- scope;
- authority;
- dependencies;
- impact;
- compatibility;
- conformance;
- migration requirements;
- supersession implications; and
- historical traceability.

### 1.6 No Silent Semantic Drift

A change that materially alters the meaning, authority, boundary, relationship, lifecycle, or interpretation of an existing canonical object shall not be treated as a merely editorial change.

Material semantic change shall require the appropriate governance treatment.

### 1.7 Traceable Decisions

Material governance decisions shall remain traceable to:

- the authority that made the decision;
- the object or subject affected;
- the decision itself;
- its rationale where required;
- its effective state;
- applicable dependencies; and
- resulting artifacts or changes.

### 1.8 Boundary Preservation

Universal Governance shall preserve explicit boundaries between:

- Universal-level authority;
- Universal Architecture;
- Universal standards;
- Universal registries;
- domain architectures;
- domain governance;
- production systems;
- implementation artifacts; and
- operational practices.

A lower-level artifact shall not acquire higher-level authority merely by reference.

### 1.9 Temporal Integrity

Universal authority shall be interpreted according to its applicable temporal state.

Current authority, historical authority, superseded authority, withdrawn authority, and deprecated material shall be distinguishable.

Historical artifacts may remain valuable as evidence or historical reference without remaining normative authority.

### 1.10 Governance Before Materialization

Where a materialization, migration, transfer, retirement, supersession, or structural change would alter Universal-level authority, the governing decision shall precede the assumption that such change has occurred.

Implementation shall not be treated as authorization.

---

## 2. Governance Authority Model

### 2.1 Purpose

The Universal Governance Authority Model defines how authority is established, exercised, delegated, constrained, recorded, and reviewed across the Universal system.

Its purpose is to prevent ambiguity regarding who or what may:

- establish normative authority;
- approve canonical artifacts;
- authorize structural or semantic change;
- establish or modify Universal-level standards;
- establish registries and registry authority;
- approve exceptions;
- resolve conflicts;
- authorize supersession or retirement; and
- determine conformance requirements.

Authority shall be explicit, bounded, and traceable.

### 2.2 Authority Is Not Equivalent to Existence

The presence of an artifact within the Universal ecosystem does not by itself establish governance authority.

The following shall not independently constitute Universal authority:

- repository presence;
- file creation;
- publication without authorization;
- implementation;
- operational usage;
- historical adoption;
- repeated citation;
- informal approval;
- individual preference; or
- derivative materialization.

Normative authority must be established through an applicable governance mechanism.

### 2.3 Authority Levels

Universal Governance recognizes the following authority levels.

#### Level 0 — Foundational Authority

Foundational Authority consists of the highest-order principles and structures that establish the identity and constitutional boundaries of the Universal system.

It establishes the conditions within which all subordinate Universal governance operates.

Foundational Authority shall not be modified through ordinary document revision.

Any change affecting foundational authority requires explicit constitutional-level governance treatment.

#### Level 1 — Universal Governance Authority

Universal Governance Authority establishes the rules by which the Universal system is governed.

It includes:

- governance principles;
- authority allocation;
- decision rights;
- change governance;
- canonicality rules;
- conformance mechanisms; and
- exception mechanisms.

Universal Governance is the principal normative instrument for these concerns.

#### Level 2 — Universal Architectural Authority

Universal Architectural Authority establishes the structural architecture of the Universal system.

Universal Architecture is the principal authority at this level.

Governance controls the evolution of this authority but does not replace it.

#### Level 3 — Universal Standard Authority

Universal Standards establish normative rules for specific Universal capabilities, concepts, naming systems, identification systems, lifecycle systems, registries, or other standardized concerns.

A Universal Standard shall operate within the authority established by Universal Governance and the structural boundaries established by Universal Architecture.

#### Level 4 — Registry Authority

A Universal Registry establishes authoritative records for the objects within its defined registry scope.

Registry authority is limited to the registry's declared ownership and semantic scope.

A registry shall not independently redefine concepts owned by a higher-level authority.

#### Level 5 — Domain Authority

Domain Authority governs a specific Universal domain, system, production environment, or specialized body of work.

Domain Authority may establish domain-specific rules where Universal-level authority has not reserved the matter and where those rules do not contradict applicable Universal authority.

#### Level 6 — Implementation Authority

Implementation artifacts translate normative requirements into executable, operational, or production-specific forms.

Implementation authority does not independently modify normative meaning.

#### Level 7 — Operational Practice

Operational Practice consists of actual usage, procedures, habits, conventions, and implementation behavior.

Operational practice may provide evidence for governance review but does not automatically become normative authority.

### 2.4 Authority Precedence

Where authorities overlap, the following precedence applies unless an explicit governing instrument establishes a narrower exception:

**Foundational Authority → Universal Governance Authority → Universal Architectural Authority → Universal Standard Authority → Registry Authority → Domain Authority → Implementation Authority → Operational Practice**

Lower-level authority shall conform to applicable higher-level authority.

A lower-level artifact cannot override higher-level authority through:

- specificity alone;
- later creation alone;
- implementation necessity;
- operational convenience;
- repository location; or
- repeated adoption.

### 2.5 Governance Roles

Universal Governance recognizes governance roles as functional authorities rather than automatically as individual persons or permanent organizational bodies.

The principal governance roles are:

- Governance Authority;
- Architecture Authority;
- Standard Authority;
- Registry Authority;
- Domain Authority;
- Conformance Authority;
- Publication Authority; and
- Decision Authority.

### 2.6 Authority Assignment

Authority shall be assigned by scope.

Every delegated authority should establish, where applicable:

1. subject matter;
2. decision class;
3. permitted action;
4. boundary;
5. prerequisites;
6. required evidence;
7. escalation condition;
8. duration or lifecycle;
9. review requirement; and
10. recording requirement.

Delegation shall not be interpreted as permanent transfer of ownership unless explicitly authorized.

### 2.7 Delegation

Universal Governance permits controlled delegation of authority.

Delegation shall:

- remain subordinate to the delegating authority;
- be explicit;
- have a defined scope;
- preserve accountability;
- remain traceable;
- be revocable where applicable; and
- not permit the delegate to redefine the authority from which the delegation originated.

### 2.8 Reserved Authority

Certain decisions shall remain reserved to Universal-level authority unless explicitly delegated.

Reserved matters include, at minimum:

- modification of Universal Governance itself;
- modification of foundational authority;
- establishment or dissolution of Universal-level authority classes;
- material alteration of Universal Architecture;
- creation or retirement of Universal-level canonical systems;
- changes affecting Universal-wide identity or authority boundaries;
- resolution of conflicts between major Universal authorities;
- authorization of exceptions with Universal-wide consequences; and
- decisions that materially alter the constitutional structure of the Universal ecosystem.

### 2.9 Decision Classes

Governance decisions shall be classified according to their materiality.

#### Class A — Editorial Decision

A change that does not materially alter normative meaning, authority, structure, relationship, lifecycle, or conformance requirements.

#### Class B — Controlled Normative Decision

A change that modifies a normative provision within an established authority boundary but does not alter the authority hierarchy or fundamental architecture.

#### Class C — Structural Decision

A decision that materially changes architecture, ownership, authority boundaries, registry structures, lifecycle structures, or major relationships.

#### Class D — Foundational Decision

A decision affecting the foundational principles, constitutional boundaries, or highest-order authority of the Universal system.

The required approval threshold shall increase with decision class.

### 2.10 Decision Rights

Decision rights shall be assigned according to the subject and materiality of the decision.

No role shall exercise decision rights merely because it is involved in:

- drafting;
- reviewing;
- implementing;
- publishing; or
- operating

the affected artifact.

Participation does not automatically constitute authority.

### 2.11 Separation of Decision, Materialization, and Publication

Universal Governance establishes three distinct governance actions:

**Decision** — Determines what is authorized.

**Materialization** — Creates or modifies the artifact that expresses the authorized decision.

**Publication** — Makes the authorized artifact formally available in its applicable published state.

These actions shall not be treated as interchangeable.

In particular:

> Implementation or materialization shall not be interpreted as governance authorization.

And:

> Publication shall not retroactively legitimize an unauthorized decision.

### 2.12 Governance Decision Record

Material governance decisions shall produce a traceable decision record containing, as applicable:

- decision identifier;
- decision date;
- decision authority;
- decision class;
- subject;
- scope;
- decision statement;
- rationale;
- affected artifacts;
- dependencies;
- effective state;
- supersession implications;
- required implementation;
- required publication;
- conformance implications; and
- subsequent review requirements.

### 2.13 Conflict Resolution

When two apparently authoritative artifacts conflict, the conflict shall not be resolved solely by selecting the newer, more detailed, or more convenient artifact.

Conflict resolution shall examine:

1. authority level;
2. scope;
3. effective status;
4. ownership;
5. temporal applicability;
6. explicit supersession;
7. delegation;
8. dependency relationships; and
9. governing decision records.

### 2.14 Governance Integrity Rule

No governance actor, document, registry, implementation, or operational practice may unilaterally elevate its own authority.

Authority must originate from an authorized governance mechanism.

## 3. Universal Governance Object Model

### 3.1 Purpose

The Universal Governance Object Model defines the classes of objects that may be governed within the Universal system and establishes the relationship between those objects and governance authority.

Its purpose is to ensure that governance is applied to identifiable objects with explicit scope, ownership, authority, lifecycle, and state.

Governance shall not operate on undefined or ambiguous targets.

### 3.2 Governance Object

A Governance Object is any identifiable entity, artifact, structure, decision, relationship, process, or state that is subject to Universal governance.

A Governance Object may be:

- normative;
- structural;
- registrational;
- decisional;
- procedural;
- evidentiary;
- operational; or
- transitional.

### 3.3 Principal Governance Object Classes

Universal Governance recognizes:

- Governance Instrument;
- Architecture Object;
- Standard Object;
- Registry Object;
- Decision Object;
- Lifecycle Object;
- Relationship Object;
- Evidence Object;
- Exception Object; and
- Change Object.

### 3.4 Governance Artifact and Governance Object

Not every Governance Object is necessarily a document.

A document may contain one or more Governance Objects.

Conversely, a Governance Object may exist as:

- a registry record;
- a structured data object;
- a decision record;
- a lifecycle state;
- a relationship;
- an approval record; or
- another formally recognized representation.

Therefore:

> Document identity and governance-object identity shall not be assumed to be equivalent.

### 3.5 Authority-Carrying Objects

An object may carry normative authority only where its authority is explicitly established.

Authority-bearing objects shall have, where applicable:

- authority source;
- owner;
- scope;
- status;
- effective date;
- version or revision;
- governing authority;
- applicable dependencies; and
- supersession or retirement state.

### 3.6 Governed Object Identity

Every Governance Object that requires persistent governance shall have a stable identity.

Identity shall support:

- unambiguous reference;
- lifecycle tracking;
- relationship tracking;
- change history;
- authority tracing;
- supersession tracing; and
- historical reconstruction.

Where an existing Universal identification or naming authority applies, that authority shall be used rather than creating a parallel identification mechanism.

### 3.7 Governance Object Ownership

Every governed object shall have an identifiable owner or owning authority.

Ownership establishes responsibility for:

- maintenance;
- interpretation within scope;
- lifecycle management;
- change initiation;
- conformance where applicable;
- conflict escalation; and
- retirement or supersession.

Ownership does not automatically grant unlimited authority.

### 3.8 Governance Object Scope

Each governed object shall have an explicit scope.

Scope may be defined by:

- subject;
- domain;
- capability;
- lifecycle;
- population of objects;
- jurisdiction within the Universal system;
- temporal applicability; or
- another formally defined boundary.

### 3.9 Governance Object State

Governed objects shall have a distinguishable governance state where lifecycle management applies.

Possible states may include:

- proposed;
- under review;
- approved;
- active;
- superseded;
- withdrawn;
- retired;
- rejected; or
- archived.

The exact lifecycle shall be defined by the governing instrument applicable to the object class.

### 3.10 Governance Object Relationships

Governance Objects shall support explicit relationships including:

| Relationship | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **governs** | establishes governance over another object |
| **authorizes** | grants authority for an action or state |
| **owns** | identifies responsible authority |
| **delegates** | transfers defined decision authority within scope |
| **depends-on** | establishes a dependency |
| **conforms-to** | establishes normative conformance |
| **implements** | identifies implementation of an authoritative requirement |
| **supersedes** | establishes replacement authority |
| **withdraws** | removes an object's active authority |
| **derives-from** | identifies authorized derivation |
| **allocates** | assigns an identity, resource, or controlled value |
| **evidences** | provides evidence supporting a claim or decision |
| **excepts** | establishes an authorized exception |
| **changes** | identifies an object affected by a governed change |

### 3.11 Governance Dependency

Where one governed object depends materially on another, the dependency shall be identifiable.

A dependency may affect:

- validity;
- authority;
- lifecycle;
- conformance;
- implementation;
- publication;
- retirement; or
- change impact.

### 3.12 Supersession

Supersession is a governed relationship in which an authorized object replaces the applicable authority or function of another object within a defined scope.

Supersession shall be explicit.

The existence of a newer document does not by itself establish supersession.

### 3.13 Withdrawal and Retirement

Withdrawal and retirement are distinct governance states.

**Withdrawal** removes or suspends active applicability according to the applicable governance decision.

**Retirement** formally ends the object's active lifecycle within its governed scope.

Neither action shall erase historical existence.

### 3.14 Governance Object Materialization

A governance decision may require one or more artifacts to be created or modified.

Materialization shall preserve traceability from:

**Governance Decision → Authorized Change → Materialized Object → Published State**

### 3.15 Governance Object Integrity

A governed object shall not be considered valid merely because its representation exists.

Where applicable, integrity requires consistency between:

- identity;
- authority;
- scope;
- owner;
- lifecycle state;
- relationships;
- version or revision;
- effective status; and
- governance decision history.

### 3.16 Governance Object Model Boundary

The Universal Governance Object Model defines what Universal Governance may govern.

It does not replace the semantic models of specialized Universal systems.

Universal Governance governs their **authority, lifecycle, relationships, change, conformance, and boundary conditions** at the applicable governance level.

It shall not unnecessarily redefine their domain-specific semantics.

---

## 4. Universal Governance Lifecycle

### 4.1 Purpose

The Universal Governance Lifecycle defines the controlled progression through which a governance matter moves from identification and proposal to authorization, materialization, publication, active governance, change, supersession, withdrawal, or retirement.

The lifecycle governs the **governance process** and does not replace the lifecycle of the governed object itself.

### 4.2 Lifecycle Separation

Universal Governance distinguishes between:

**Governance Lifecycle** — the lifecycle of a governance matter, decision, or change.

**Object Lifecycle** — the lifecycle of the artifact or object affected by the governance decision.

**Operational Lifecycle** — the lifecycle through which an implementation or operational system uses the governed object.

### 4.3 Governance Lifecycle States

The primary states are:

1. **Identified**
2. **Proposed**
3. **Under Review**
4. **Decision Pending**
5. **Authorized**
6. **Materializing**
7. **Validated**
8. **Published**
9. **Active**
10. **Superseded**
11. **Withdrawn**
12. **Retired**

Additional states may include:

- **Rejected**
- **Cancelled**
- **Deferred**

### 4.4 Identified

A governance matter enters **Identified** when a potential governance need has been recognized.

Identification does not constitute a proposal or authorization.

### 4.5 Proposed

A matter enters **Proposed** when a concrete governance action has been formulated.

A proposal should identify:

- subject;
- requested action;
- affected objects;
- scope;
- rationale;
- expected impact;
- dependencies;
- proposed authority;
- proposed decision class; and
- required implementation.

### 4.6 Under Review

A proposal enters **Under Review** when relevant authorities evaluate validity and impact.

Review may include:

- architectural review;
- semantic review;
- governance review;
- dependency analysis;
- conformance analysis;
- lifecycle analysis;
- impact assessment;
- compatibility analysis; and
- historical authority analysis.

### 4.7 Decision Pending

A matter enters **Decision Pending** when required analysis has been completed sufficiently for the designated Decision Authority to determine the outcome.

Possible outcomes include:

- approve;
- approve with conditions;
- reject;
- defer;
- return for revision; or
- escalate.

### 4.8 Authorized

A matter enters **Authorized** only after the competent Decision Authority has formally approved the applicable action.

Authorization shall establish, where applicable:

- authorized action;
- authority;
- scope;
- affected objects;
- effective condition or date;
- required materialization;
- required validation;
- publication requirements;
- conformance implications; and
- applicable exceptions or conditions.

### 4.9 Materializing

A matter enters **Materializing** when the authorized decision is being expressed in required artifacts, records, relationships, or system changes.

Materialization shall remain within the scope of authorization.

### 4.10 Validated

A matter enters **Validated** when the materialized result has been evaluated against the requirements established by the authorization.

Validation may include:

- structural validation;
- semantic validation;
- governance validation;
- registry validation;
- conformance validation;
- dependency validation;
- identity validation;
- lifecycle validation; and
- publication readiness validation.

### 4.11 Published

A matter enters **Published** when the authorized and validated result has been formally released through the applicable publication mechanism.

Publication shall not retroactively authorize an unauthorized change.

### 4.12 Active

A governed result enters **Active** when it is formally applicable within its defined scope.

Therefore:

**Published ≠ automatically Active.**

### 4.13 Superseded

A governed object enters **Superseded** when another authorized object has replaced its applicable authority or function within a defined scope.

Supersession shall require explicit authorization.

### 4.14 Withdrawn

A governed object enters **Withdrawn** when its active applicability is removed or suspended through an authorized governance action.

### 4.15 Retired

A governed object enters **Retired** when its active lifecycle has been formally terminated.

Retirement shall identify, where applicable:

- retirement authority;
- effective date;
- reason;
- successor;
- migration requirements;
- residual dependencies; and
- historical preservation requirements.

### 4.16 Rejected, Cancelled, and Deferred

**Rejected** means the proposed action was not authorized.

**Cancelled** means the governance process was intentionally terminated before authorization.

**Deferred** means a decision was intentionally postponed while preserving the matter for possible future action.

### 4.17 Permitted Transition Principle

The general progression is:

**Identified → Proposed → Under Review → Decision Pending → Authorized → Materializing → Validated → Published → Active**

Subsequent authorized events may result in:

**Superseded**, **Withdrawn**, or **Retired**.

A matter may also transition to **Rejected**, **Cancelled**, or **Deferred** where applicable.

### 4.18 No Implicit Transition

No lifecycle transition shall be inferred solely from:

- file creation;
- file modification;
- repository commit;
- implementation;
- publication;
- citation;
- operational use; or
- passage of time.

### 4.19 Effective State

Governance shall distinguish between:

- decision state;
- publication state;
- effective state; and
- operational adoption.

These states may occur at different times.

### 4.20 Governance Change Event

Every material lifecycle transition shall be associated with a Governance Change Event identifying, where applicable:

- event identifier;
- affected object;
- previous state;
- new state;
- triggering decision;
- responsible authority;
- timestamp or effective date;
- applicable conditions; and
- resulting artifacts.

### 4.21 Correction

Where a published artifact contains an error, correction shall be governed explicitly.

Corrections shall distinguish between:

- editorial correction;
- normative correction;
- structural correction; and
- emergency correction.

### 4.22 Emergency Governance

An emergency governance mechanism may be established for situations where immediate action is required to protect the integrity, security, continuity, legality, or fundamental coherence of the Universal system.

Emergency action shall:

- identify the emergency basis;
- identify the authority exercising emergency power;
- define the temporary scope;
- minimize deviation from ordinary governance;
- preserve evidence;
- establish a review requirement; and
- undergo post-action normalization where required.

### 4.23 Existing Universal Lifecycles

Where a Universal system already has a specialized lifecycle, Universal Governance governs that lifecycle at the appropriate authority level without replacing its domain-specific semantics.

### 4.24 Historical Preservation

Universal Governance shall preserve sufficient historical information to reconstruct:

- what authority existed;
- when it existed;
- who authorized it;
- what it governed;
- what replaced it;
- what changed; and
- why the relevant governance state changed where rationale is required.

### 4.25 Lifecycle Integrity

A governance lifecycle shall maintain consistency between:

- governance decision;
- authority;
- object identity;
- lifecycle state;
- effective status;
- publication status;
- relationships;
- dependencies; and
- historical record.

---

## 5. Universal Change Governance Framework

### 5.1 Purpose

The Universal Change Governance Framework establishes the rules by which changes to governed objects are proposed, classified, assessed, authorized, materialized, validated, published, and incorporated into the Universal system.

### 5.2 Change as a Governed Event

A change is any authorized or proposed action that may alter the identity, meaning, authority, structure, relationship, lifecycle, applicability, conformance, or operational interpretation of a governed object.

Changes include:

- creation;
- amendment;
- revision;
- correction;
- migration;
- transfer;
- reallocation;
- delegation;
- supersession;
- withdrawal;
- retirement;
- restoration; and
- controlled reactivation.

### 5.3 Change Classification

#### Editorial Change

Modifies presentation without changing normative meaning.

#### Clarifying Change

Improves explicitness or removes ambiguity without intentionally changing underlying normative meaning.

#### Normative Change

Modifies a requirement, rule, authority, definition, relationship, lifecycle condition, applicability condition, or other normative meaning.

#### Structural Change

Alters organization or architecture of a governed system.

#### Foundational Change

Affects Universal-level constitutional principles, foundational authority, or fundamental boundaries.

### 5.4 Change Materiality

Change classification shall be based on materiality.

A change is material when it may affect one or more of:

- normative meaning;
- authority;
- scope;
- ownership;
- identity;
- architecture;
- lifecycle;
- conformance;
- dependencies;
- interoperability;
- canonical status;
- publication state; or
- historical interpretation.

### 5.5 Change Impact Assessment

Material changes shall undergo impact assessment proportionate to classification.

The assessment shall consider, where applicable:

1. architectural impact;
2. semantic impact;
3. governance impact;
4. authority impact;
5. registry impact;
6. lifecycle impact;
7. dependency impact;
8. conformance impact;
9. implementation impact;
10. publication impact;
11. migration impact;
12. historical impact; and
13. downstream domain impact.

### 5.6 Architectural Impact

A change has architectural impact when it may alter:

- Universal Architecture;
- architectural boundaries;
- architectural ownership;
- structural relationships;
- architectural principles;
- system composition; or
- architectural dependencies.

Where architectural impact exists, the applicable Architecture Authority shall review it.

### 5.7 Semantic Impact

A change has semantic impact when it changes meaning, interpretation, definition, relationship, or normative behavior.

A one-word change may constitute a major semantic change.

### 5.8 Authority Impact

A change has authority impact when it changes:

- ownership;
- decision rights;
- authoritative artifact;
- authority precedence;
- delegation;
- applicability;
- canonical status; or
- governance boundaries.

### 5.9 Registry Impact

A change has registry impact when it affects:

- registry ownership;
- registry schema;
- allocation rules;
- object identity;
- registry state;
- registration semantics;
- registry relationships; or
- authority represented by registry records.

### 5.10 Lifecycle Impact

A change has lifecycle impact when it changes:

- allowed states;
- transition rules;
- transition authority;
- effective conditions;
- retirement behavior;
- supersession; or
- withdrawal.

### 5.11 Dependency Impact

Material changes shall identify dependencies that may be:

- broken;
- invalidated;
- reinterpreted;
- migrated;
- superseded; or
- newly introduced.

### 5.12 Downstream Impact

Universal-level changes shall be assessed for impact on subordinate systems and domains, including standards, registries, production systems, implementations, documentation, conformance procedures, identifiers, workflows, and operations.

### 5.13 Change Proposal Requirements

A material change proposal should contain:

- change identifier;
- initiating authority;
- affected object(s);
- current state;
- proposed state;
- change classification;
- rationale;
- scope;
- impact assessment;
- dependencies;
- migration requirements;
- implementation implications;
- validation requirements;
- publication requirements; and
- proposed effective date or condition.

### 5.14 Change Approval Threshold

| Change Class | Minimum Governance Treatment |
|---|---|
| Editorial | Authorized editorial review |
| Clarifying | Semantic-equivalence review |
| Normative | Explicit normative authorization |
| Structural | Normative authorization + architectural review where applicable |
| Foundational | Highest applicable Universal authority |

### 5.15 Multi-Authority Changes

A change affecting multiple authority domains shall establish:

- participating authorities;
- lead Decision Authority;
- decision dependencies;
- required concurrence;
- escalation path; and
- final authority.

Consultation, concurrence, and approval shall remain distinguishable.

### 5.16 Migration Governance

Migration shall identify:

- predecessor;
- successor;
- migration scope;
- migration method;
- affected dependencies;
- transitional state;
- effective date;
- validation;
- rollback or recovery where applicable; and
- completion criteria.

Migration shall not itself be interpreted as supersession unless supersession has been authorized.

### 5.17 Transfer of Authority

Transfer of authority is a distinct governance event.

A transfer shall identify:

- originating authority;
- receiving authority;
- transferred scope;
- effective condition;
- affected objects;
- retained responsibilities;
- registry implications; and
- authorization.

Copying, moving, renaming, or rehosting a document does not constitute authority transfer.

### 5.18 Materialization Control

Authorized changes shall be materialized according to approved scope.

Materialization shall not:

- add unauthorized requirements;
- remove authorized requirements;
- alter authority;
- change identity without authorization;
- introduce hidden dependencies; or
- silently expand scope.

### 5.19 Validation Before Activation

Material changes shall be validated before activation where required.

Validation shall confirm, as applicable:

- authorized scope;
- semantic integrity;
- structural integrity;
- identity integrity;
- relationship integrity;
- dependency integrity;
- lifecycle integrity;
- conformance;
- publication readiness; and
- migration readiness.

### 5.20 Rollback and Recovery

Where a change may create material operational or governance risk, rollback or recovery requirements should be defined where feasible.

Rollback shall itself be governed.

Reverting implementation does not automatically revert the governing decision.

### 5.21 Post-Change Review

Material changes shall be subject to post-change review where required.

### 5.22 Change Failure

If a change fails during materialization, validation, migration, or activation, governance shall explicitly determine the resulting state.

Possible outcomes include:

- correction;
- retry;
- rollback;
- reauthorization;
- suspension;
- withdrawal;
- cancellation; or
- new change proposal.

### 5.23 Change Traceability

Every material change shall maintain traceability across:

**Change Proposal → Review → Decision → Authorization → Materialization → Validation → Publication → Effective State → Operational Adoption**

Where applicable:

**→ Supersession / Withdrawal / Retirement**

### 5.24 Change History

Change history shall preserve:

- previous state;
- changed state;
- effective timing;
- decision authority;
- material change classification;
- affected objects;
- relevant dependencies; and
- resulting artifacts.

### 5.25 Prohibition of Retroactive Authorization

A change already implemented without authorization shall not be legitimized merely by subsequently publishing or approving the resulting artifact.

Where unauthorized change is discovered, governance shall determine appropriate treatment.

---

## 6. Universal Conformance and Compliance Governance Framework

### 6.1 Purpose

This framework establishes how the Universal system determines, records, evaluates, remediates, and governs conformity and compliance.

It distinguishes:

**Conformance** — whether an object, system, process, or implementation satisfies an applicable normative requirement.

**Compliance** — whether an actor, process, system, or governance activity follows applicable governance obligations, controls, and authorized procedures.

### 6.2 Conformance

Conformance is a normative relationship between a subject and an applicable requirement.

### 6.3 Compliance

Compliance concerns adherence to applicable governance obligations.

An implementation may be technically conformant while the governance process used to create it was non-compliant.

Conversely, a governance process may be compliant while the resulting implementation fails a substantive technical or semantic requirement.

### 6.4 Conformance Target

A Conformance Target is an identifiable subject against which a normative requirement is evaluated.

It may be:

- a document;
- a standard;
- a registry;
- a registry record;
- an architecture;
- a system;
- a process;
- an implementation;
- a workflow;
- a governance activity; or
- another governed object.

### 6.5 Normative Basis

Every conformance determination shall identify:

- requirement;
- authoritative source;
- applicable version or revision;
- scope;
- effective state;
- applicable exceptions; and
- assessment criteria.

### 6.6 Conformance Assessment

A Conformance Assessment evaluates a target against an applicable normative basis.

Assessment may include:

- document review;
- structural inspection;
- semantic analysis;
- registry verification;
- lifecycle verification;
- implementation testing;
- evidence review;
- relationship verification; or
- other authorized methods.

### 6.7 Conformance Status

Possible statuses include:

- **Conformant**
- **Partially Conformant**
- **Non-Conformant**
- **Not Applicable**
- **Unable to Determine**
- **Pending Assessment**

### 6.8 Partial Conformance

Partial conformance indicates that some applicable requirements are satisfied while one or more remain unmet.

Partial conformance shall not be represented as full conformance.

### 6.9 Non-Conformance

Non-conformance occurs when a target fails an applicable normative requirement.

A finding shall identify, where applicable:

- target;
- requirement;
- evidence;
- nature of deviation;
- severity;
- impact;
- responsible owner;
- remediation requirement; and
- resolution status.

### 6.10 Governance Non-Compliance

Governance non-compliance occurs when an actor, process, or system fails to follow an applicable governance obligation.

Examples include:

- unauthorized change;
- unauthorized publication;
- authority exceeded through delegation;
- missing required approval;
- lifecycle transition without authorization;
- failure to preserve required evidence;
- improper authority transfer; or
- bypassing mandatory governance review.

### 6.11 Conformance and Compliance Relationship

| Situation | Conformance | Compliance |
|---|---|---|
| Correct artifact, unauthorized creation | May conform | Non-compliant |
| Authorized artifact, substantive requirement violated | Non-conformant | May be compliant |
| Authorized and technically correct | Conformant | Compliant |
| Evidence insufficient | Undetermined | May also be undetermined |
| Explicit exception applies | Assessed against exception | Governed according to exception |

### 6.12 Evidence

Conformance and compliance determinations shall be evidence-based.

Evidence may include:

- approved documents;
- governance decisions;
- registry records;
- lifecycle records;
- validation results;
- implementation outputs;
- test results;
- review records;
- audit records;
- change records; and
- other authorized evidence.

### 6.13 Evidence Sufficiency

Evidence shall be evaluated for:

- authenticity;
- relevance;
- completeness;
- temporal validity;
- authority;
- consistency; and
- traceability.

### 6.14 Conformance Authority

The Conformance Authority determines whether a target conforms to an applicable requirement according to the authorized assessment mechanism.

It shall not rewrite the requirement or create new normative obligations through assessment.

### 6.15 Compliance Authority

The Compliance Authority evaluates adherence to governance obligations.

Where no dedicated Compliance Authority exists, the applicable Governance Authority may perform or delegate the function.

### 6.16 Findings

A finding may include:

- non-conformance;
- governance non-compliance;
- control weakness;
- evidence deficiency;
- unauthorized change;
- lifecycle inconsistency;
- authority conflict; or
- other governance-relevant condition.

### 6.17 Severity

Possible severity levels include:

- informational;
- minor;
- moderate;
- major; and
- critical.

Severity shall consider materiality and impact.

### 6.18 Remediation

Remediation may include:

- correction;
- re-materialization;
- migration;
- additional validation;
- restoration;
- authority clarification;
- lifecycle correction;
- withdrawal;
- retirement;
- process correction; or
- other authorized action.

### 6.19 Remediation Tracking

Material findings shall have traceable remediation status.

### 6.20 Exception Governance

An exception permits a defined deviation from an applicable requirement under explicit authorization.

Exceptions shall be:

- explicit;
- scoped;
- justified;
- authorized;
- time-bounded where appropriate;
- traceable;
- reviewable; and
- revocable where applicable.

### 6.21 Exception Conditions

An exception should identify:

- affected requirement;
- affected target;
- deviation;
- reason;
- approving authority;
- duration;
- conditions;
- compensating controls;
- review requirements; and
- termination condition.

### 6.22 Exception and Conformance

Where a valid exception applies, conformance shall be evaluated against the requirement as modified by the authorized exception mechanism.

Therefore:

**Requirement + Authorized Exception = Governed Deviation**

The exception does not amend the underlying requirement.

### 6.23 Exception and Compliance

Exception authorization does not automatically waive unrelated governance obligations.

### 6.24 Enforcement

Enforcement is the application of authorized governance consequences following a material compliance or conformance issue.

Possible consequences may include:

- remediation;
- suspension;
- withdrawal;
- restriction;
- escalation;
- revalidation;
- depublication where authorized;
- retirement; or
- other proportionate measures.

### 6.25 Enforcement Boundary

No assessment authority may create enforcement powers merely by declaring a finding.

### 6.26 Conformance Claims

A claim of conformance shall identify, where material:

- conformance target;
- normative basis;
- assessment date;
- assessment authority;
- applicable version;
- assessment scope;
- exceptions;
- findings; and
- resulting status.

### 6.27 Compliance Claims

A compliance claim shall identify, where material:

- applicable governance obligation;
- compliance subject;
- assessment scope;
- evidence;
- assessment authority;
- exceptions;
- findings; and
- status.

### 6.28 Continuous Conformance

Where a requirement is inherently continuous, conformance shall not be assumed permanently from a historical assessment.

### 6.29 Change-Triggered Reassessment

A material change may invalidate a previous conformance or compliance determination.

### 6.30 Conformance Records

Material conformance and compliance assessments shall be preserved as governance evidence.

### 6.31 Governance Integrity Rule

Conformance assessment shall determine whether an existing requirement is satisfied.

It shall not be used to retroactively legitimize an unauthorized change.

Likewise, compliance assessment shall not be used to silently alter normative requirements.

---

## 7. Universal Governance Exception, Escalation, and Conflict Resolution Framework

### 7.1 Purpose

This framework establishes mechanisms by which governance uncertainty, authority disputes, conflicting normative sources, unresolved exceptions, deadlocks, and governance failures are identified, escalated, evaluated, and resolved.

### 7.2 Governance Escalation

Escalation is the controlled transfer of a governance matter to a higher or otherwise competent authority because the current authority cannot resolve the matter within its assigned scope.

### 7.3 Escalation Does Not Transfer Ownership Automatically

Escalation of a matter does not automatically transfer ownership of the affected governed object.

The distinction shall be maintained between:

- ownership of the object;
- responsibility for the matter;
- authority to decide the matter; and
- authority to execute the resulting decision.

### 7.4 Escalation Levels

The conceptual escalation path is:

**Operational / Implementation Level → Domain Authority → Standard or Registry Authority → Architecture / Governance Authority → Universal Governance Authority → Foundational Authority where applicable**

### 7.5 Escalation Triggers

Mandatory escalation shall occur when a matter:

1. exceeds delegated authority;
2. affects multiple independent authority domains;
3. creates a material authority conflict;
4. materially affects Universal Architecture;
5. affects foundational boundaries;
6. creates unresolved normative contradiction;
7. requires an exception beyond delegated authority;
8. creates systemic conformance or compliance risk;
9. cannot be resolved within the applicable decision period; or
10. presents an emergency requiring immediate higher-level intervention.

### 7.6 Governance Conflict

A Governance Conflict exists when two or more apparently applicable authorities, decisions, artifacts, requirements, or interpretations cannot simultaneously be applied without contradiction.

### 7.7 Conflict Identification

A conflict record shall identify, where applicable:

- conflicting objects;
- respective authorities;
- scope;
- effective states;
- temporal applicability;
- relevant relationships;
- dependencies;
- apparent contradiction;
- affected systems or domains; and
- immediate governance risk.

### 7.8 Conflict Resolution Order

Conflicts shall be evaluated using:

1. explicit authority;
2. authority level;
3. scope;
4. effective status;
5. temporal applicability;
6. explicit supersession;
7. delegation;
8. ownership;
9. dependency;
10. specificity within authorized scope; and
11. operational implementation.

### 7.9 Specificity Rule

A more specific rule does not automatically override a higher-level rule.

### 7.10 Recency Rule

A newer artifact does not automatically supersede an older authoritative artifact.

### 7.11 Historical Authority Rule

An older artifact may remain historically authoritative for a previous period even after it ceases to be current.

### 7.12 Conflict Between Governance and Architecture

Where Universal Governance appears to conflict with Universal Architecture, the conflict shall be explicitly assessed rather than informally resolving it through revision.

### 7.13 Conflict Between Universal and Domain Authority

Where a domain rule conflicts with Universal authority, Universal authority prevails within the scope reserved to Universal governance.

Where Universal authority intentionally delegates a matter to the domain, domain authority prevails within the delegated scope.

### 7.14 Governance Deadlock

A Governance Deadlock exists when competent authorities cannot reach a decision required for continued governance.

A deadlock shall be documented rather than resolved through unilateral action.

### 7.15 Deadlock Resolution

Deadlocks may be resolved through:

- clarification of authority;
- narrowing of scope;
- additional evidence;
- independent review;
- mediation;
- escalation;
- designated final decision authority; or
- foundational determination.

### 7.16 Final Decision Authority

Where ordinary mechanisms cannot resolve a material conflict, a Final Decision Authority may be invoked where established.

Final Decision Authority shall operate within authorized scope.

### 7.17 Exception Escalation

An exception shall be escalated when:

- it exceeds delegated authority;
- it affects multiple domains;
- it affects Universal Architecture;
- it creates systemic risk;
- it conflicts with another exception;
- it becomes indefinite without explicit authorization;
- it materially changes governance behavior; or
- the exception authority cannot determine applicability.

### 7.18 Exception Accumulation

Multiple exceptions shall not be used to create an informal alternative governance regime.

Repeated exceptions may indicate that the underlying requirement requires review.

### 7.19 Temporary Resolution

Where immediate permanent resolution is impossible, an interim governance position may be established if authorized.

It shall define scope, duration or review condition, authority, and required subsequent resolution.

### 7.20 Emergency Escalation

Emergency escalation may be invoked where delay would create material risk to:

- Universal integrity;
- governance authority;
- architectural integrity;
- operational continuity;
- legal or regulatory obligations;
- security;
- critical dependencies; or
- other recognized fundamental interests.

### 7.21 Emergency Override

Where emergency authority permits temporary deviation from ordinary governance, the deviation shall identify:

- overridden requirement;
- emergency authority;
- scope;
- duration;
- evidence; and
- post-emergency review.

### 7.22 Post-Emergency Normalization

After an emergency condition ends, governance shall determine whether:

- ordinary governance should resume;
- temporary action should be reversed;
- a permanent change should be proposed;
- an exception should be established; or
- architectural/foundational review is required.

### 7.23 Unauthorized Action During Conflict

When a conflict exists, participants shall not independently select the authority they prefer and act upon it where the matter exceeds their authority.

### 7.24 Preservation of Competing Positions

Material conflicts shall preserve relevant competing positions where necessary for historical reconstruction.

### 7.25 Conflict Resolution Record

A material conflict resolution shall record:

- conflict identifier;
- affected objects;
- competing authorities;
- scope;
- applicable rules;
- evidence;
- analysis;
- decision authority;
- resolution;
- effective date;
- consequences; and
- required changes.

### 7.26 Governance Precedent

A resolved conflict may provide governance precedent.

Precedent does not automatically become normative authority.

### 7.27 Interpretation

Interpretation determines how an existing authoritative provision applies to a specific circumstance without changing normative meaning.

Where interpretation necessarily changes meaning, the matter shall be treated as normative change.

### 7.28 Governance Clarification

Clarification may resolve ambiguity where underlying authority is already established.

### 7.29 No Governance by Silence

Silence, absence of objection, historical non-enforcement, or operational practice shall not automatically establish approval, delegation, exception, supersession, authority transfer, or conformance.

### 7.30 No Governance by Accumulation

Repeated implementation, repeated citation, repeated exception, or repeated operational practice shall not independently create Universal authority.

### 7.31 Resolution Integrity

A governance resolution shall be complete only when competent authority has acted, scope is established, affected objects are identified, resulting state is clear, required materialization is defined, effective status is established, dependencies are addressed, and the decision is recorded.

---

## 8. Universal Governance Lifecycle — Additional Control Provisions

The lifecycle controls established in Section 4 shall also govern:

- correction;
- emergency action;
- historical preservation;
- state reconstruction;
- specialized subordinate lifecycles; and
- effective-state distinctions.

Where a subordinate system has a specialized lifecycle, that lifecycle remains semantically owned by its governing system while Universal Governance controls authority, change, conformance, exception, and boundary conditions at the appropriate level.

---

## 9. Universal Change Governance — Additional Control Provisions

The change framework shall preserve:

- explicit authority transfer;
- migration governance;
- materialization control;
- validation before activation;
- rollback/recovery where applicable;
- post-change review;
- change history; and
- prohibition of retroactive authorization.

These controls are cumulative with the lifecycle and canonicality frameworks.

---

## 10. Universal Conformance and Compliance — Additional Control Provisions

Conformance and compliance shall remain:

- evidence-based;
- scoped;
- temporally valid;
- explicitly tied to normative authority;
- distinct from one another; and
- subject to remediation and exception governance.

---

## 11. Universal Governance Exception, Escalation, and Conflict Resolution — Additional Control Provisions

Exception, escalation, and conflict mechanisms shall preserve:

- authority hierarchy;
- explicit scope;
- historical traceability;
- separation of interpretation from normative change;
- temporary versus permanent resolution;
- emergency normalization; and
- prohibition of governance by silence or accumulation.

---

## 12. Universal Canonicality, Document Authority, Publication, Supersession, and Retirement Framework

### 12.1 Purpose

This framework establishes how Universal documents and other authoritative artifacts acquire, retain, change, lose, and preserve normative authority.

### 12.2 Document as a Governance Artifact

A document may function as a governance artifact when it expresses:

- normative requirements;
- architectural definitions;
- governance rules;
- standards;
- registry definitions;
- lifecycle rules;
- decisions;
- procedures; or
- other authorized Universal content.

Not every Universal document is normative.

### 12.3 Document Identity

Every document requiring persistent governance shall possess a stable identity.

### 12.4 Document Authority

Document authority is the formally established normative authority carried by a document within a defined scope.

A document may be:

- authoritative;
- informative;
- historical;
- superseded;
- withdrawn;
- retired; or
- pending authorization.

### 12.5 Canonicality

Canonicality is the formally recognized status of an artifact as the authoritative representation of a defined Universal subject within its declared scope.

Canonicality shall not be inferred merely from publication, repository location, filename, title, age, repeated usage, completeness, implementation, citation, or preference.

### 12.6 Canonical Object

A Canonical Object is a governed object formally recognized as authoritative for its declared subject and scope.

### 12.7 Single Canonical Authority

Where Universal Governance establishes that a subject shall have a single canonical authority, multiple documents shall not simultaneously claim the same authority for the same scope unless scope is explicitly partitioned.

### 12.8 Canonicality and Versioning

A canonical document may have multiple revisions over its lifecycle.

Revision shall not automatically create a new canonical subject.

### 12.9 Canonicality and Publication

Publication and canonicality are distinct.

**Authorization establishes authority.**

**Canonical designation establishes authoritative representation.**

**Publication establishes formal availability.**

**Effective activation establishes applicability.**

### 12.10 Canonicality Determination

Canonicality determination shall consider:

- subject ownership;
- authority source;
- scope;
- governance authorization;
- relationship to existing canonical objects;
- semantic completeness;
- architectural compatibility;
- dependencies;
- lifecycle state; and
- supersession status.

### 12.11 Canonical Publication

A canonical artifact shall be published through an authorized publication mechanism.

### 12.12 Publication Integrity

The published representation shall materially correspond to the authorized representation.

### 12.13 Canonical Source and Derivatives

A canonical artifact may have derivative representations, including:

- implementation documentation;
- summaries;
- translations;
- rendered versions;
- operational guides;
- machine-readable representations; or
- downstream adaptations.

A derivative does not become canonical merely because it is more convenient, recent, or widely used.

### 12.14 Derivative Authority

A derivative may carry delegated or inherited authority only where explicitly permitted.

### 12.15 Canonicality Conflict

Where two artifacts claim canonical authority over the same subject and scope, the conflict shall be governed explicitly.

### 12.16 Supersession

Supersession is the authorized replacement of an existing canonical or authoritative object by another object within a defined scope.

### 12.17 Supersession Does Not Erase History

When an object is superseded:

- its identity remains;
- its historical state remains;
- its previous authority remains reconstructable;
- its successor remains linked to it; and
- historical references remain interpretable.

### 12.18 Partial Supersession

Supersession may be scope-limited by section, subject, authority scope, domain, lifecycle period, or other explicit boundary.

### 12.19 Withdrawal

Withdrawal removes or suspends active applicability without necessarily establishing a successor.

### 12.20 Retirement

Retirement formally terminates an artifact's active lifecycle.

Retirement does not imply deletion.

### 12.21 Reinstatement

A withdrawn or retired object may be reinstated only through explicit governance authorization.

### 12.22 Historical Reference Status

A document may remain valuable as historical reference after losing active authority.

Possible determinations include:

- currently authoritative;
- authoritative for a historical period;
- superseded;
- withdrawn;
- retired;
- incorporated into another authority;
- partially superseded; or
- reference-only.

### 12.23 Legacy Artifact Assessment

Before using a legacy artifact as normative reference, governance shall determine whether its substance has been retained, incorporated, migrated, replaced, superseded, withdrawn, retired, or otherwise accounted for by a later authoritative artifact.

### 12.24 Canonicality Review

Canonical artifacts may be reviewed for continuing authority, scope, dependency integrity, conformance, lifecycle status, supersession, retirement conditions, and architectural compatibility.

### 12.25 Authority Preservation During Revision

A revision of a canonical artifact preserves authority only where the revision has been authorized.

### 12.26 Draft Status

Draft artifacts are non-authoritative unless an explicit governance mechanism establishes otherwise.

### 12.27 Publication Repository

A repository may be designated as the official publication location for a class of artifacts.

Repository designation does not itself create canonicality.

### 12.28 Copy and Mirror Rule

Copies, mirrors, exports, backups, and synchronized representations do not independently acquire canonical authority.

### 12.29 Canonicality and Machine-Readable Representations

A machine-readable representation may be canonical where explicitly authorized.

Where human-readable and machine-readable representations both carry authority, governance shall define their scopes, equivalence requirements, precedence, synchronization, and validation.

### 12.30 Canonicality and Governance Decisions

Canonical status shall remain traceable to the governance decision that established or modified it.

The minimum conceptual relationship is:

**Governance Decision → Authority → Canonical Object → Published Representation → Effective State**

### 12.31 No Canonicality by Usage

Operational adoption, widespread citation, or long-term usage shall not independently establish canonicality.

### 12.32 No Canonicality by Recency

A newer document shall not automatically become canonical.

### 12.33 No Canonicality by Repository Position

Location within a repository, directory structure, branch, release, or documentation site shall not independently establish normative authority.

### 12.34 Canonicality Integrity

A canonical artifact shall remain consistent with its governing decision, identity, authority, scope, lifecycle, relationships, and effective status.

---

## 13. Universal Governance Roles, Responsibilities, Decision Rights, and Accountability Framework

### 13.1 Purpose

This framework defines the functional governance roles through which Universal authority is exercised, maintained, delegated, reviewed, and held accountable.

### 13.2 Role-Based Governance

Universal Governance shall define authority primarily by role and scope, not by individual identity.

### 13.3 Governance Role Categories

Principal role categories are:

1. Governance Authority;
2. Architecture Authority;
3. Standard Authority;
4. Registry Authority;
5. Domain Authority;
6. Conformance Authority;
7. Compliance Authority;
8. Publication Authority;
9. Decision Authority;
10. Review Authority;
11. Custodial Authority; and
12. Implementation Authority.

### 13.4 Governance Authority

Responsible for maintaining Universal governance itself, including governance principles, authority assignment, decision mechanisms, governance changes, conflicts, and continuity.

### 13.5 Architecture Authority

Responsible for architectural interpretation, review, impact assessment, architectural change evaluation, dependencies, and architectural conflicts.

### 13.6 Standard Authority

Responsible for Universal Standards within assigned scope, including development, review, maintenance, lifecycle, conformance requirements, and supersession.

### 13.7 Registry Authority

Responsible for authoritative registry records within defined scope, including registry integrity, registration, allocation where authorized, lifecycle, state, relationships, and auditability.

### 13.8 Domain Authority

Responsible for governance within a defined Universal domain or subordinate system, within delegated authority.

### 13.9 Conformance Authority

Responsible for assessing whether a target satisfies an applicable normative requirement.

### 13.10 Compliance Authority

Responsible for assessing adherence to governance obligations.

### 13.11 Publication Authority

Responsible for formal publication of authorized artifacts.

### 13.12 Decision Authority

Responsible for making a specific governance decision within assigned decision class and scope.

### 13.13 Review Authority

Responsible for formal evaluation of proposed decisions, changes, artifacts, or governance conditions.

### 13.14 Custodial Authority

Responsible for integrity and availability of governed records and artifacts.

### 13.15 Implementation Authority

Responsible for translating authorized requirements into operational or technical implementation.

### 13.16 Responsibility vs Authority

Responsibility and authority shall remain distinct.

A role may maintain an artifact without having authority to redefine it.

A role may approve a change without implementing it.

### 13.17 Accountability

Accountability means that a governance role remains answerable for decisions or actions performed within assigned authority.

Delegation of execution does not automatically eliminate accountability of the delegating authority.

### 13.18 Separation of Duties

Where material governance risk exists, proposing, reviewing, approving, materializing, validating, and publishing should be separated.

### 13.19 Proposal Authority

May initiate a governance change or decision but does not thereby gain approval authority.

### 13.20 Approval Authority

Formally authorizes a proposed action within assigned decision class and scope.

### 13.21 Validation Authority

Determines whether a materialized result satisfies the conditions established by authorization.

### 13.22 Publication Authority Boundary

May publish only artifacts satisfying applicable authorization and publication conditions.

### 13.23 Delegated Authority

Delegated authority shall be formally recorded with delegating authority, receiving authority, subject, decision class, scope, conditions, duration, limitations, escalation, and revocation conditions.

### 13.24 Temporary Delegation

Temporary delegation shall define start condition, end condition, scope, limitations, accountability, reporting, and restoration.

### 13.25 Authority Conflict Between Roles

Conflicts shall be resolved through explicit authority assignment, hierarchy, scope, delegation, reserved authority, decision class, and escalation.

### 13.26 Governance Responsibility Matrix

Where operationally necessary, a responsibility matrix may identify:

- governance function;
- responsible role;
- accountable role;
- required reviewers;
- decision authority;
- implementation authority; and
- publication authority.

### 13.27 Decision Rights Matrix

A decision rights matrix may map decision classes against roles:

| Function | Proposal | Review | Decision | Materialize | Validate | Publish |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Editorial | R | R | A | R | R | A |
| Normative | R | R | A | R | A | A |
| Structural | R | A/R | A | R | A | A |
| Foundational | R | A/R | A | R | A | A |

**R = Responsible / participating**

**A = Authorized / accountable decision role**

This matrix remains illustrative until specific authority assignments are formally established.

### 13.28 Governance Continuity

Governance shall remain operable through personnel changes, temporary authority unavailability, and other continuity conditions.

### 13.29 Governance Capacity

Work may be distributed through delegated review, delegated decision rights, domain authorities, registry authorities, specialist reviewers, or procedural automation.

### 13.30 Automated Governance

Automation may execute governance functions where explicitly authorized, but remains subordinate to applicable authority and decision rules.

### 13.31 Governance Evidence Responsibility

Every governance role shall preserve evidence necessary to demonstrate actions performed within its authority.

### 13.32 Role Assignment

Role assignment shall identify, where applicable:

- role;
- assignee;
- scope;
- authority;
- effective period;
- delegated powers;
- restrictions;
- accountability;
- successor or alternate; and
- revocation conditions.

### 13.33 Role Revocation

A role may be revoked, suspended, or replaced through authorized governance action.

### 13.34 Role Independence

Where independence is required, the authority performing a function shall be sufficiently independent from the authority being evaluated.

### 13.35 Governance Accountability Boundary

No role shall be accountable for outcomes outside its authorized scope merely because it participated in the process.

---

## 14. Universal Governance Records, Evidence, Traceability, and Audit Framework

### 14.1 Purpose

This framework establishes requirements for creating, maintaining, preserving, and using governance records and evidence necessary to demonstrate the integrity of Universal governance.

### 14.2 Governance Record

A Governance Record is an authoritative or evidentiary record created to document a governance event, state, decision, assessment, relationship, authorization, exception, or other governed activity.

### 14.3 Governance Evidence

Governance Evidence is information used to substantiate a governance claim, decision, state, relationship, or assessment.

### 14.4 Evidence vs Authority

Evidence and authority shall remain distinct.

An artifact may prove that a decision occurred without itself becoming the normative authority governing the matter.

### 14.5 Minimum Governance Record

A material governance record should identify, where applicable:

- record identifier;
- subject;
- object(s) affected;
- event or decision;
- authority;
- responsible role;
- date or timestamp;
- effective condition;
- state;
- scope;
- evidence;
- dependencies;
- resulting action;
- resulting artifact;
- supersession or retirement relationship; and
- applicable review status.

### 14.6 Decision Record

A material governance decision shall have a traceable Decision Record establishing what was decided, who had authority, what was affected, why where required, when it was decided, when effective, what was authorized, and what constraints applied.

### 14.7 Decision Evidence

Decision evidence may include proposal, impact assessment, architectural assessment, semantic analysis, conformance analysis, dependency analysis, review comments, authority assignment, prior decisions, and historical artifacts.

### 14.8 Approval Record

Approval records identify approving authority, approved subject, scope, decision class, approval state, effective timing, and conditions.

### 14.9 Review Record

Review records identify reviewer, subject, scope, criteria, findings, recommendations, unresolved issues, and outcome.

### 14.10 Change Record

A material change shall link:

**Previous State → Change Decision → New State**

### 14.11 Lifecycle Record

A Lifecycle Record documents a material state transition and its authority, trigger, effective time, conditions, relationships, and evidence.

### 14.12 Conformance Record

A Conformance Record establishes target, normative basis, assessment authority, scope, date, evidence, findings, exceptions, status, and reassessment requirements.

### 14.13 Compliance Record

A Compliance Record establishes governance obligation, subject, assessment authority, scope, evidence, findings, exceptions, status, and remediation.

### 14.14 Exception Record

An Exception Record identifies requirement, target, deviation, justification, authority, scope, duration, conditions, compensating controls, review condition, and termination.

### 14.15 Conflict Resolution Record

A material conflict resolution records the conflict, affected authorities, competing interpretations, rules, evidence, analysis, final authority, resolution, and effective state.

### 14.16 Delegation Record

A delegation record identifies delegating authority, receiving authority, scope, decision class, limitations, effective period, accountability, and revocation.

### 14.17 Publication Record

A publication record identifies published object, identity, version/revision, authorization, publication authority, timestamp, effective state, representation, validation, and predecessor/successor where applicable.

### 14.18 Supersession Record

A supersession record identifies predecessor, successor, authority, scope, effective date, migration requirement, residual applicability, and historical status.

### 14.19 Withdrawal Record

A withdrawal record identifies withdrawn object, authority, reason, scope, timing, affected consumers, temporary/permanent nature, replacement, and restoration conditions where applicable.

### 14.20 Retirement Record

A retirement record identifies retired object, authority, timing, reason, successor, migration status, residual dependencies, and preservation requirements.

### 14.21 Traceability Chain

Material Universal governance shall maintain traceability capable of linking:

**Object → Proposal → Review → Decision → Authorization → Materialization → Validation → Publication → Effective State → Conformance / Compliance → Change History → Supersession / Withdrawal / Retirement**

### 14.22 Bidirectional Traceability

Where practical, traceability shall operate both from decision to affected objects and from object to decisions that established or changed it.

### 14.23 Temporal Traceability

Records shall preserve sufficient temporal information to determine what was authoritative at a given time.

### 14.24 Provenance

Material governance records shall preserve provenance where necessary, including origin, source, derivation, transformation, migration, transfer, materialization, publication, and modification.

### 14.25 Evidence Integrity

Governance evidence shall be protected against unauthorized alteration where integrity is material.

### 14.26 Evidence Retention

Material governance records shall be retained long enough to preserve authority, decision, lifecycle, conformance, compliance, supersession, and required legal or operational evidence.

### 14.27 Evidence Preservation During Supersession

Supersession shall not destroy supporting evidence.

### 14.28 Evidence Preservation During Retirement

Retirement shall not automatically permit destruction of governance evidence.

### 14.29 Audit

Audit is systematic examination of governance records, evidence, controls, and outcomes.

### 14.30 Audit Independence

Where required, auditors shall be independent of the activity being audited.

### 14.31 Audit Scope

Audit scope shall be explicitly defined and shall not implicitly expand beyond its authorized mandate.

### 14.32 Audit Finding

An audit finding may concern non-compliance, non-conformance, control weakness, unauthorized action, missing evidence, inconsistent lifecycle state, authority ambiguity, canonicality conflict, or other governance deficiency.

### 14.33 Audit Remediation

Findings requiring action shall produce traceable remediation and validation.

### 14.34 Audit Trail

The Universal governance system shall maintain an audit trail sufficient to reconstruct material governance activity.

### 14.35 Auditability by Design

Governed systems should generate the evidence required for later review during architecture, governance, registry, lifecycle, implementation, publication, and change design.

### 14.36 Missing Evidence

Where required evidence is missing, governance shall determine whether the appropriate conclusion is evidence deficiency, unable to determine, non-compliance, non-conformance, or another applicable finding.

### 14.37 Reconstruction

Universal Governance shall support reconstruction of a governed object's material history.

### 14.38 Governance Transparency

Governance information should be available to authorized stakeholders at a level sufficient to understand authority, decisions, canonicality, pending changes, exceptions, and material findings, subject to applicable access controls.

### 14.39 Audit and Historical Authority

Audit shall distinguish current authority from historical, superseded, withdrawn, retired, and non-authoritative material.

### 14.40 Governance Record Integrity

Governance Records shall not silently overwrite historical governance state where preservation is required.

### 14.41 Governance Records and Registries

Where an authorized registry records governance objects or events, registry authority remains limited to its declared scope.

### 14.42 Governance Records and Documents

Documents may contain governance records, but storage alone does not guarantee governance traceability.

### 14.43 Governance Audit Frequency

Audit frequency shall be determined according to governance risk, change frequency, authority criticality, historical findings, dependency complexity, lifecycle sensitivity, and applicable requirements.

### 14.44 Audit Trigger Events

Audit or governance review may be triggered by:

- material architectural change;
- authority conflict;
- major non-conformance;
- unauthorized change;
- repeated exceptions;
