Such matters shall be handled through applicable governance mechanisms.

### 33.40 Conflict of Interest in Participation

Stakeholders participating in material governance matters should disclose conflicts where required.

Conflict does not necessarily prevent participation, but may require:

- disclosure;
- recusal;
- restricted participation;
- independent review; or
- other controls.

### 33.41 Expert Participation

Subject-matter experts may be consulted where their knowledge materially improves governance quality.

Expertise does not automatically create decision authority.

### 33.42 Independent Expertise

Where a matter is contested or high-risk, governance may seek independent expertise.

Independent expertise should be sufficiently free from conflicts relevant to the question.

### 33.43 Minority or Dissenting View

Where governance decisions involve material disagreement, a dissenting or minority view may be recorded where appropriate.

Recording dissent does not prevent the authorized decision from becoming effective.

### 33.44 Dissent Record

A material dissent record may identify:

- position;
- evidence;
- affected scope;
- rationale;
- response by decision authority; and
- resulting decision.

### 33.45 Consensus Building

Governance may use consensus-building mechanisms to:

- clarify interests;
- identify common ground;
- reduce conflict;
- develop alternatives; or
- improve decision quality.

Consensus-building shall not bypass formal authority.

### 33.46 Stakeholder Prioritization

Where stakeholder interests conflict, prioritization should consider:

- authority;
- legal or regulatory obligations where applicable;
- risk;
- impact;
- rights or legitimate interests;
- dependency;
- proportionality;
- strategic purpose; and
- applicable Universal principles.

### 33.47 No Popularity-Based Authority

The number of stakeholders supporting a position does not automatically determine the governance outcome.

Authority remains determined by the applicable governance mechanism.

### 33.48 Stakeholder Impact Assessment

Material governance changes should assess:

- affected stakeholders;
- type of impact;
- magnitude;
- duration;
- reversibility;
- dependency;
- required mitigation; and
- communication needs.

### 33.49 Stakeholder Impact Categories

Impact may include:

- rights;
- obligations;
- access;
- cost;
- workload;
- implementation;
- security;
- privacy;
- continuity;
- interoperability; or
- governance participation.

### 33.50 Vulnerable or High-Impact Stakeholders

Where a governance decision may disproportionately affect stakeholders with limited ability to respond, governance should consider additional:

- consultation;
- notice;
- mitigation;
- representation;
- accessibility; or
- review.

### 33.51 Stakeholder Dependency

A stakeholder may depend on:

- an active standard;
- identifier;
- registry;
- lifecycle state;
- publication;
- implementation;
- decision; or
- governance service.

Material dependency should be identified before disruptive change.

### 33.52 Consumer Notification

Consumers of normative or canonical artifacts should receive notification of material changes affecting:

- applicability;
- identity;
- semantics;
- compatibility;
- lifecycle;
- conformance;
- migration; or
- effective state.

### 33.53 Consumer Migration Support

Where a material change creates migration requirements, governance should provide appropriate:

- transition information;
- timelines;
- compatibility information;
- mapping;
- examples;
- validation mechanisms; or
- support channels.

### 33.54 Stakeholder Participation During Emergency

Emergency governance may reduce normal consultation requirements where necessary.

Any reduction shall be:

- authorized;
- proportionate;
- recorded;
- limited to the emergency; and
- followed by appropriate post-event review.

### 33.55 Emergency Communication

Material emergency decisions should be communicated as rapidly as practical.

Emergency communication shall distinguish:

- temporary state;
- authoritative decision;
- expected duration;
- required action; and
- follow-up process.

### 33.56 Stakeholder Participation During Recovery

Where continuity or recovery affects stakeholders, governance should communicate:

- current state;
- temporary limitations;
- expected recovery;
- authoritative source;
- required action; and
- reconciliation process.

### 33.57 Stakeholder Participation and Privacy

Stakeholder engagement mechanisms shall minimize unnecessary collection or exposure of personal information.

### 33.58 Stakeholder Participation Records

Material participation records should preserve:

- participant identity where appropriate;
- role;
- submission;
- date;
- subject;
- response;
- decision treatment; and
- applicable confidentiality.

### 33.59 Participation Record Retention

Retention shall follow applicable records requirements.

Material participation evidence should remain available for historical reconstruction where necessary.

### 33.60 Stakeholder Relationship Mapping

Governance may maintain relationships such as:

- affected by;
- depends on;
- represents;
- consults;
- informs;
- challenges;
- approves;
- implements; or
- consumes.

Relationship data shall not create authority unless explicitly defined.

### 33.61 Stakeholder Lifecycle

Stakeholder participation relationships may have lifecycle states such as:

- identified;
- eligible;
- invited;
- participating;
- inactive;
- suspended;
- replaced; or
- closed.

### 33.62 Participation Rights Lifecycle

Participation rights may change through:

- appointment;
- delegation;
- role change;
- scope change;
- conflict;
- suspension;
- expiration; or
- governance change.

### 33.63 Stakeholder Representation Changes

Changes in representation shall preserve:

- predecessor;
- successor;
- mandate;
- effective time;
- outstanding matters; and
- historical records.

### 33.64 Stakeholder Engagement Planning

Material governance initiatives should define an engagement plan covering:

- stakeholder groups;
- objectives;
- timing;
- methods;
- information;
- participation rights;
- response handling;
- escalation; and
- records.

### 33.65 Engagement Proportionality

Engagement should be proportionate to:

- impact;
- risk;
- scope;
- complexity;
- reversibility;
- stakeholder diversity; and
- governance materiality.

### 33.66 Engagement Effectiveness

Governance may evaluate whether engagement:

- reached relevant stakeholders;
- produced useful evidence;
- identified material risks;
- reduced ambiguity;
- improved decision quality; and
- supported implementation.

High participation volume does not necessarily indicate effective engagement.

### 33.67 Stakeholder Fatigue

Repeated or unnecessary consultation may reduce meaningful participation.

Governance should avoid:

- duplicate requests;
- unnecessary meetings;
- repetitive submissions;
- unclear decision purpose; and
- consultations with no defined governance use.

### 33.68 Consultation Reuse

Where a prior consultation remains materially relevant, its evidence may be reused if:

- scope remains valid;
- assumptions remain valid;
- stakeholders remain materially representative; and
- temporal validity is preserved.

### 33.69 Stakeholder Disagreement

Stakeholder disagreement should be distinguished from governance conflict.

Disagreement becomes a governance conflict when it materially challenges:

- authority;
- interpretation;
- decision;
- requirement;
- applicability; or
- another governed state.

### 33.70 Stakeholder Escalation

Stakeholders should have an appropriate route to escalate material concerns where:

- local response fails;
- authority ambiguity exists;
- material harm is alleged;
- evidence is ignored;
- procedural fairness is compromised; or
- systemic risk is identified.

### 33.71 Stakeholder Escalation Boundaries

Escalation shall not automatically create decision authority for the stakeholder.

### 33.72 Stakeholder Governance Transparency

Governance should make sufficiently clear:

- who has authority;
- how decisions are made;
- how stakeholders may participate;
- where authoritative information is found;
- how challenges are raised; and
- how concerns are escalated.

### 33.73 Stakeholder Information Quality

Information provided to stakeholders should be:

- accurate;
- current;
- relevant;
- understandable;
- appropriately complete; and
- clearly distinguished between authoritative and informative content.

### 33.74 Stakeholder Communication Correction

Where materially incorrect governance communication is discovered, correction should be issued promptly.

Correction shall identify the authoritative state.

### 33.75 Stakeholder and Canonicality

Stakeholder consensus does not establish canonicality.

Canonicality shall remain determined by the applicable authority and lifecycle mechanisms.

### 33.76 Stakeholder and Normativity

Stakeholder preference does not itself create or repeal normative requirements.

Normative change requires the applicable authority.

### 33.77 Stakeholder and Decision Authority

Stakeholder participation may inform a decision but does not substitute for the designated decision authority.

### 33.78 Stakeholder and Conformance

Stakeholder feedback may provide evidence of implementation or impact but shall not itself establish conformance status.

### 33.79 Stakeholder and Evidence

Material stakeholder submissions may constitute governance evidence where their provenance, relevance, and reliability are appropriate.

### 33.80 Stakeholder and Dispute Resolution

Stakeholder participation in dispute resolution shall follow the applicable standing, confidentiality, evidence, and authority rules.

### 33.81 Stakeholder Metrics

Governance may monitor:

- participation coverage;
- consultation response rate;
- stakeholder diversity;
- unresolved concerns;
- response time;
- representation coverage;
- repeated concerns;
- stakeholder impact;
- communication reach; and
- engagement effectiveness.

Metrics are management information unless explicitly made normative.

### 33.82 Stakeholder Anti-Gaming

Governance shall guard against:

- manufacturing apparent consensus;
- excluding relevant stakeholders to accelerate approval;
- using consultation as symbolic compliance;
- overwhelming decision processes with duplicate submissions;
- treating participant volume as authority;
- suppressing dissent;
- selectively reporting feedback;
- misrepresenting stakeholder representation; and
- using confidentiality to conceal legitimate governance concerns.

### 33.83 Stakeholder, Participation, Consultation, and Representation Status

This section establishes the initial Universal Governance Stakeholder, Participation, Consultation, and Representation Framework.

It remains subject to validation against the final authority hierarchy, participation rights model, dispute framework, privacy and information governance, decision framework, and applicable organizational requirements.

It is **canonically locked as part of Universal Governance v1.0**.


---

## 34. Universal Governance Information, Records, Documentation, and Evidence Management Framework

### 34.1 Purpose

This framework establishes how governance information, records, documentation, metadata, evidence, and authoritative representations are created, classified, maintained, protected, retrieved, shared, preserved, and disposed of.

Its purpose is to ensure that governance information remains:

- accurate;
- attributable;
- authoritative where applicable;
- traceable;
- available to authorized users;
- protected from unauthorized alteration;
- temporally reliable; and
- sufficient for governance continuity and historical reconstruction.

### 34.2 Governance Information

Governance Information is information used to establish, operate, evidence, interpret, administer, or evaluate governance.

It may include:

- decisions;
- policies;
- standards;
- requirements;
- records;
- registries;
- evidence;
- metadata;
- communications;
- assessments;
- audit records;
- lifecycle state;
- authority assignments; and
- implementation records.

### 34.3 Governance Record

A Governance Record is a retained representation of a governance event, decision, state, transaction, obligation, assessment, communication, or other material activity.

A record shall be preserved according to its required retention and integrity needs.

### 34.4 Governance Document

A Governance Document is a structured representation of governance information intended for human or machine consumption.

A document may be:

- normative;
- informative;
- evidentiary;
- procedural;
- architectural;
- administrative; or
- another defined class.

### 34.5 Document vs Record

A document is a representation.

A record is retained evidence of an event, state, transaction, or governance fact.

A document may become a record when retained for evidentiary or historical purposes.

### 34.6 Evidence

Evidence is information capable of supporting a governance conclusion, decision, assessment, or historical determination.

Evidence shall be evaluated for:

- relevance;
- sufficiency;
- reliability;
- provenance;
- integrity;
- temporal validity; and
- scope.

### 34.7 Metadata

Metadata describes an object, record, document, or evidence item.

Material metadata may include:

- identity;
- type;
- owner;
- authority;
- version;
- lifecycle state;
- effective time;
- creation time;
- modification time;
- source;
- classification;
- relationships; and
- retention.

### 34.8 Metadata Authority

Not all metadata fields have equal authority.

Governance shall identify which metadata sources are authoritative for:

- identity;
- lifecycle;
- ownership;
- version;
- publication;
- effective state;
- classification; and
- other material attributes.

### 34.9 Record Identity

Material records shall have stable identity sufficient to support:

- retrieval;
- reference;
- audit;
- relationship;
- retention;
- disposition; and
- historical reconstruction.

### 34.10 Record Provenance

Material records should preserve provenance indicating:

- source;
- creator or originating system where appropriate;
- creation event;
- transformation;
- transfer;
- custody;
- publication;
- modification; and
- disposition.

### 34.11 Record Authenticity

Records shall be managed so that authorized users can establish reasonable confidence that the record is what it purports to be.

### 34.12 Record Integrity

Material records shall be protected from unauthorized:

- alteration;
- deletion;
- replacement;
- backdating;
- identity reassignment; or
- selective history removal.

### 34.13 Record Completeness

Record completeness means that sufficient information exists to understand the relevant governance event or state.

Completeness does not require retaining every incidental artifact.

### 34.14 Record Context

A material record should preserve sufficient context to establish:

- subject;
- scope;
- authority;
- time;
- relationship;
- purpose;
- outcome; and
- applicable state.

### 34.15 Record Classification

Governance information may be classified by:

- normative status;
- confidentiality;
- sensitivity;
- evidentiary value;
- operational importance;
- retention requirement; or
- other authorized classification.

Classification shall not itself establish authority.

### 34.16 Information Sensitivity

Sensitive governance information may require additional controls for:

- access;
- transmission;
- storage;
- publication;
- retention; and
- disposal.

### 34.17 Information Access

Access shall be granted according to:

- role;
- authority;
- need;
- scope;
- confidentiality;
- security;
- privacy; and
- applicable restrictions.

Technical access shall not automatically establish authority to interpret or change the information.

### 34.18 Least Privilege

Access to material governance information should follow least privilege where practical.

Users should receive only the access necessary for their authorized function.

### 34.19 Segregation of Information Duties

Where material risk exists, creation, approval, publication, modification, and deletion functions should be appropriately separated.

### 34.20 Canonical Information Source

Where an authoritative source exists, it shall be identifiable.

Copies, caches, exports, mirrors, and derivative representations shall not automatically become authoritative.

### 34.21 Source of Truth

A Source of Truth is a designated authoritative information source for a defined class of information.

Source-of-truth status shall be:

- explicit;
- scoped;
- governed;
- discoverable; and
- change-controlled.

### 34.22 Multiple Sources of Truth

Different systems may each be authoritative for different information classes.

Governance shall avoid ambiguous overlap for the same authoritative attribute.

### 34.23 Authoritative Field

A field or attribute may have an explicit authoritative source even where the containing document or system is not globally authoritative.

### 34.24 Document Control

Controlled governance documents should maintain:

- identity;
- version;
- owner;
- authority;
- lifecycle;
- approval;
- publication;
- effective state;
- change history; and
- references.

### 34.25 Document Versioning

Versioning shall distinguish:

- content revision;
- lifecycle state;
- publication instance;
- copy;
- translation; and
- derivative representation.

### 34.26 Document Revision

Material document revisions shall be classified according to their effect, including:

- editorial;
- clarifying;
- normative;
- structural;
- foundational.

The applicable change mechanism shall follow the classification.

### 34.27 Document Approval

Approval shall identify:

- authority;
- subject;
- version;
- date;
- conditions;
- effective state; and
- evidence.

### 34.28 Document Publication

Publication shall establish a controlled public or authorized distribution state.

Publication shall preserve:

- identity;
- version;
- provenance;
- authority;
- publication time;
- effective state; and
- canonical source.

### 34.29 Document Retraction

A published document may be retracted where authorized.

Retraction shall preserve historical evidence and identify:

- authority;
- reason;
- affected version;
- effective time; and
- replacement or correction where applicable.

### 34.30 Document Correction

Corrections shall preserve historical integrity.

A correction should identify:

- original;
- error;
- correction;
- authority;
- effective time; and
- resulting state.

### 34.31 Translation

A translated governance document shall preserve its relationship to the source document.

Where only one language version is authoritative, that status shall be explicit.

### 34.32 Localization

Localized documentation shall identify:

- source;
- localization scope;
- authority;
- deviations;
- effective state; and
- relationship to the source.

### 34.33 Derivative Document

A derivative document is generated from another authoritative source.

The derivative shall preserve:

- source identity;
- derivation relationship;
- generation time;
- applicable version;
- transformation; and
- limitations.

### 34.34 Machine-Readable Representation

Where governance information is represented in machine-readable form, the machine representation should preserve semantic equivalence with the authoritative source where required.

### 34.35 Human-Readable Representation

Human-readable representations should preserve enough semantic information for authorized consumers to understand the governed state.

### 34.36 Semantic Equivalence

Different representations may be considered equivalent only where governance defines the equivalence criteria.

Formatting similarity alone does not establish semantic equivalence.

### 34.37 Documentation Hierarchy

Documentation may be organized by:

- foundational;
- architectural;
- normative;
- procedural;
- evidentiary;
- informative; and
- operational

layers.

The hierarchy shall not create authority beyond the underlying authority model.

### 34.38 Documentation Dependency

Documents may depend on:

- policies;
- standards;
- registries;
- architecture;
- decisions;
- definitions;
- evidence; or
- external sources.

Dependencies should be traceable where material.

### 34.39 Broken Reference

A broken reference exists when a documented relationship points to an unavailable, invalid, ambiguous, or incorrect target.

Material broken references shall be corrected or explicitly handled.

### 34.40 Reference Stability

References to material governance objects should use stable identity rather than transient presentation details where possible.

### 34.41 Evidence Collection

Evidence collection shall be:

- authorized;
- relevant;
- proportionate;
- traceable;
- appropriately protected; and
- retained according to requirements.

### 34.42 Evidence Capture

Evidence should be captured sufficiently close to the relevant event where temporal accuracy is material.

### 34.43 Evidence Transformation

Where evidence is transformed, the transformation should preserve:

- source;
- transformation method;
- time;
- operator or system;
- integrity;
- resulting representation; and
- relationship to the original.

### 34.44 Evidence Copy

A copy may be used as evidence where authenticity and integrity can be established.

### 34.45 Evidence Chain of Custody

Material evidence should preserve custody history where needed to establish integrity.

### 34.46 Evidence Retention

Evidence shall be retained for as long as required by:

- governance;
- audit;
- legal or regulatory obligation where applicable;
- risk;
- historical reconstruction; or
- another authorized requirement.

### 34.47 Evidence Expiration

Evidence may become unsuitable for a purpose because:

- it is stale;
- source state changed;
- scope changed;
- integrity cannot be established;
- criteria changed; or
- retention conditions expired.

### 34.48 Evidence Reuse

Evidence may be reused where:

- purpose is compatible;
- scope remains applicable;
- integrity is preserved;
- temporal validity is sufficient; and
- reuse does not misrepresent its original context.

### 34.49 Evidence Destruction

Material evidence shall not be destroyed while a valid retention requirement, audit, dispute, investigation, or legal hold applies.

### 34.50 Legal or Governance Hold

A hold temporarily prevents disposal of defined information.

A hold shall identify:

- scope;
- authority;
- start;
- review;
- affected records; and
- release condition.

### 34.51 Retention Schedule

Governance may define retention periods based on:

- record class;
- materiality;
- legal requirements where applicable;
- audit needs;
- risk;
- historical value; and
- operational necessity.

### 34.52 Retention Exceptions

Exceptions to retention periods require explicit authorization.

### 34.53 Disposition

Disposition is the authorized final treatment of a record after its retention requirement ends.

Disposition may include:

- destruction;
- deletion;
- anonymization where appropriate;
- transfer;
- archival; or
- another authorized treatment.

### 34.54 Disposition Authorization

Material disposition shall be authorized according to the applicable records authority.

### 34.55 Disposition Evidence

Disposition should preserve evidence of:

- record class;
- scope;
- authorization;
- date;
- method;
- responsible party; and
- resulting state.

### 34.56 Immutable Record

An immutable record is a record whose historical content cannot be altered without detection or whose alteration is prevented by the applicable control.

Immutability shall not prevent authorized successor records or corrections.

### 34.57 Append-Only History

Where appropriate, governance records should use append-only history to preserve:

- original event;
- subsequent correction;
- transition;
- decision;
- interpretation; and
- closure.

### 34.58 Historical Reconstruction

Material governance systems should support reconstruction of:

- what existed;
- what was authoritative;
- who acted;
- under what authority;
- what evidence existed;
- what state applied; and
- when it applied.

### 34.59 As-Of Information

Governance should support queries equivalent to:

> “What was the authoritative record or state at time T?”

As-of reconstruction shall account for temporal relationships among:

- identity;
- version;
- lifecycle;
- authority;
- publication;
- evidence;
- decisions; and
- records.

### 34.60 Record Synchronization

Where multiple systems maintain copies of governance records, synchronization should preserve:

- identity;
- version;
- lifecycle;
- authority;
- integrity; and
- effective state.

### 34.61 Record Conflict

A record conflict exists where multiple representations claim incompatible authoritative facts.

Resolution shall use the designated source of truth and applicable authority.

### 34.62 Record Reconciliation

Reconciliation shall preserve:

- original conflicting states;
- authoritative determination;
- correction;
- affected scope; and
- historical evidence.

### 34.63 Record Migration

Migration of governance records shall preserve, where required:

- identity;
- relationships;
- provenance;
- lifecycle;
- timestamps;
- authority;
- evidence;
- access controls; and
- historical continuity.

### 34.64 Record Conversion

Format conversion shall preserve semantic content and material metadata.

Conversion shall be validated where loss of information is possible.

### 34.65 Record Export

Exports should identify:

- source;
- extraction time;
- scope;
- version;
- filters;
- transformation; and
- limitations.

### 34.66 Record Import

Imported governance records shall be assessed for:

- identity;
- provenance;
- authority;
- integrity;
- compatibility;
- lifecycle;
- duplicates; and
- historical continuity.

### 34.67 Record Duplication

Duplicate records may create ambiguity.

Governance should identify:

- canonical record;
- duplicate relationship;
- purpose of duplicate;
- synchronization requirements; and
- retention treatment.

### 34.68 Record Orphan

An orphan record lacks a valid relationship to its governing object, authority, or context.

Material orphan records should be reconciled or explicitly classified.

### 34.69 Record Linkage

Material records should be linked to:

- decisions;
- authorities;
- objects;
- lifecycle states;
- evidence;
- changes;
- findings; and
- publications

where those relationships are required for traceability.

### 34.70 Record Access Logging

Access to sensitive or material records may require logging of:

- user or system;
- time;
- action;
- object;
- result; and
- relevant authorization.

### 34.71 Record Change Logging

Material record changes should preserve:

- actor;
- authority;
- time;
- previous state;
- new state;
- reason; and
- evidence.

### 34.72 Unauthorized Record Change

An unauthorized record change shall be treated as a governance integrity issue.

Response should include:

1. containment;
2. evidence preservation;
3. authoritative state determination;
4. correction;
5. access review; and
6. post-event assessment.

### 34.73 Records During Emergency

Emergency operations shall preserve material governance records to the greatest practical extent.

Emergency shortcuts shall not justify intentional historical erasure.

### 34.74 Records During Recovery

Recovery shall reconcile:

- authoritative records;
- restored copies;
- pending transactions;
- lifecycle state;
- decisions;
- evidence; and
- changes.

### 34.75 Records and Confidentiality

Confidentiality controls shall coexist with historical integrity.

Restricting access shall not mean deleting or rewriting records.

### 34.76 Records and Privacy

Governance records containing personal information shall be managed according to applicable privacy requirements.

Retention and disclosure shall be proportionate to legitimate governance purposes.

### 34.77 Records and Security

Material records should be protected against:

- unauthorized access;
- alteration;
- destruction;
- exfiltration;
- corruption; and
- availability loss.

### 34.78 Records and Conformance

Records may provide evidence of:

- authority;
- lifecycle;
- implementation;
- assessment;
- approval;
- publication;
- change; or
- compliance.

### 34.79 Records and Audit

Audit activities shall preserve sufficient records to support:

- audit trail;
- findings;
- conclusions;
- remediation;
- closure; and
- later review.

### 34.80 Records and Disputes

Material dispute records shall preserve:

- positions;
- evidence;
- decisions;
- communications;
- interpretations;
- resolution; and
- appeal where applicable.

### 34.81 Records and Lifecycle

Records shall preserve lifecycle transitions where those transitions are material to authority or applicability.

### 34.82 Records and Change Management

Change records should link:

**Change → Decision → Authority → Affected Object → Evidence → Implementation → Validation → Effective State**

### 34.83 Records and Publication

Publication records should establish:

- artifact;
- version;
- authority;
- publication channel;
- publication time;
- effective state;
- canonical source; and
- withdrawal or correction history.

### 34.84 Records and Naming and Identification

Record identity shall follow the applicable Universal identification authority.

Record filenames shall not be treated as authoritative identity unless explicitly designated.

### 34.85 Documentation Quality

Material governance documentation should be:

- accurate;
- current;
- internally consistent;
- traceable;
- understandable;
- accessible to authorized consumers;
- maintainable; and
- appropriately structured.

### 34.86 Documentation Review

Controlled documentation should be reviewed when:

- normative requirements change;
- architecture changes;
- dependencies change;
- material findings arise;
- references break;
- lifecycle changes;
- ownership changes; or
- review becomes due.

### 34.87 Documentation Owner

Each controlled governance document should have an accountable owner responsible for:

- maintenance;
- review;
- reference integrity;
- lifecycle;
- publication;
- correction; and
- retirement.

### 34.88 Documentation Consumer

Consumers should be able to determine:

- document identity;
- authoritative version;
- current state;
- applicability;
- source;
- dependencies; and
- limitations.

### 34.89 Documentation Discoverability

Material documents and records should be discoverable through controlled mechanisms appropriate to their classification.

### 34.90 Documentation Integrity

Governance shall guard against:

- unauthorized editing;
- hidden replacement;
- stale copies;
- misleading filenames;
- incorrect metadata;
- broken links;
- version ambiguity; and
- provenance loss.

### 34.91 Information and Record Metrics

Governance may monitor:

- missing records;
- broken references;
- stale documents;
- duplicate records;
- orphan records;
- unauthorized changes;
- retention exceptions;
- disposition backlog;
- evidence gaps;
- metadata quality; and
- retrieval performance.

Metrics are management information unless explicitly made normative.

### 34.92 Information, Records, Documentation, and Evidence Anti-Gaming

Governance shall guard against:

- deleting unfavorable history;
- selectively preserving evidence;
- backdating records;
- changing metadata to alter historical meaning;
- presenting copies as canonical;
- using formatting changes to conceal substantive changes;
- destroying evidence during disputes;
- retaining excessive irrelevant information to obscure material evidence; and
- claiming traceability without preserving the underlying relationship.

### 34.93 Information, Records, Documentation, and Evidence Status

This section establishes the initial Universal Governance Information, Records, Documentation, and Evidence Management Framework.

It remains subject to validation against the final information governance architecture, evidence framework, records lifecycle, security and privacy requirements, naming and identification system, publication model, and audit framework.

It is **canonically locked as part of Universal Governance v1.0**.


---

## 35. Universal Governance Operating Model

### 35.1 Purpose

This framework defines how Universal Governance operates as an integrated governance system.

It establishes the relationship among:

- governance authorities;
- governance roles;
- governance capabilities;
- governance processes;
- governance services;
- governance forums;
- governance inputs and outputs;
- governance information;
- governance decisions;
- governance controls;
- governance feedback; and
- governance improvement.

This framework operationalizes Universal Governance without redefining the normative authority established by the Universal Governance authority model.

### 35.2 Governance Operating Model

The Governance Operating Model is the structured representation of how governance capabilities, authorities, processes, people, information, controls, and decision mechanisms operate together to produce governed outcomes.

It shall remain consistent with Universal Architecture and applicable Universal Governance instruments.

### 35.3 Operating Model Boundary

The operating model shall describe governance operation without replacing:

- Universal Architecture;
- governance principles;
- authority hierarchy;
- normative instruments;
- lifecycle semantics;
- object identity;
- domain-specific operating procedures; or
- implementation architecture.

### 35.4 Governance Inputs

Governance inputs may include:

- objectives;
- proposals;
- requirements;
- evidence;
- risks;
- stakeholder input;
- performance information;
- lifecycle events;
- change requests;
- findings;
- incidents;
- external obligations; and
- governance signals.

### 35.5 Governance Activities

Governance activities may include:

- planning;
- assessment;
- consultation;
- decision-making;
- authorization;
- allocation;
- monitoring;
- assurance;
- escalation;
- review;
- communication;
- change;
- lifecycle management; and
- improvement.

### 35.6 Governance Outputs

Governance outputs may include:

- decisions;
- approvals;
- authorizations;
- policies;
- standards;
- requirements;
- allocations;
- lifecycle transitions;
- exceptions;
- findings;
- corrective actions;
- publications;
- stakeholder communications; and
- governance performance outcomes.

### 35.7 Governance Capability

A Governance Capability is the organizational or systemic ability to perform a defined governance function reliably within authorized scope.

Capabilities may include:

- authority management;
- decision governance;
- lifecycle governance;
- change governance;
- risk governance;
- conformance;
- assurance;
- stakeholder governance;
- information governance; and
- strategic governance.

### 35.8 Capability vs Process

A capability describes an ability.

A process describes how an activity is performed.

A capability may be implemented through multiple processes.

### 35.9 Governance Process

A Governance Process is a defined sequence of governance activities that transforms inputs into controlled outputs.

Processes shall operate within applicable authority, lifecycle, normative, and control boundaries.

### 35.10 Governance Service

A Governance Service is a repeatable governance function made available to authorized consumers.

Examples may include:

- decision submission;
- conformance assessment;
- identity registration;
- publication;
- exception processing;
- dispute handling;
- governance reporting; or
- lifecycle transition.

### 35.11 Governance Service vs Process

A service represents a governance capability offered to a consumer.

A process represents the internal activity sequence used to deliver an outcome.

### 35.12 Governance Forum

A Governance Forum is a defined mechanism through which governance participants collectively perform one or more governance functions.

A forum may be:

- a committee;
- council;
- review board;
- working group;
- designated meeting;
- digital decision mechanism; or
- another authorized structure.

### 35.13 Forum Authority

A forum shall have explicitly defined:

- purpose;
- authority;
- membership;
- quorum;
- decision rights;
- escalation;
- records;
- lifecycle; and
- scope.

Participation in a forum does not itself confer authority beyond the forum's defined mandate.

### 35.14 Governance Role in the Operating Model

Roles shall be mapped to:

- responsibilities;
- authorities;
- processes;
- services;
- decision points;
- controls; and
- escalation paths.

### 35.15 Separation of Duties

Where material risk exists, the operating model should separate:

- proposal;
- assessment;
- approval;
- authorization;
- implementation;
- publication; and
- assurance.

Where separation is impractical, compensating controls shall apply.

### 35.16 Governance Decision Flow

A material governance flow may follow:

**Input → Analysis → Consultation → Decision → Authorization → Materialization → Implementation → Validation → Monitoring → Review**

The exact flow shall depend on the governed matter.

### 35.17 Decision Point

A Decision Point is a defined point in a governance process where an authorized authority must determine an outcome.

Decision points should identify:

- authority;
- input;
- criteria;
- possible outcomes;
- evidence;
- conditions; and
- escalation.

### 35.18 Control Point

A Control Point is a defined point where governance verifies that a required condition is satisfied before proceeding.

Control points may address:

- authority;
- conformance;
- evidence;
- security;
- lifecycle;
- quality;
- dependency; or
- risk.

### 35.19 Escalation Point

An Escalation Point identifies when a matter must move to a higher or specialized authority.

Escalation conditions shall be explicit where material.

### 35.20 Governance Workflow

A Governance Workflow coordinates governance activities, participants, decisions, states, evidence, and outputs.

A workflow shall not redefine canonical lifecycle semantics.

### 35.21 Workflow Orchestration

Governance workflows may be orchestrated through:

- human processes;
- systems;
- registries;
- workflow engines;
- controlled communications; or
- combinations thereof.

Automation shall not alter authority unless explicitly authorized.

### 35.22 Human vs Automated Governance

Automation may execute authorized governance activities.

Automated execution shall not create authority that the underlying governance model does not provide.

### 35.23 Governance Automation

Governance automation should preserve:

- authority verification;
- decision traceability;
- evidence;
- lifecycle;
- identity;
- auditability;
- exception handling; and
- human escalation where required.

### 35.24 Governance Information Flow

The operating model shall identify how material information moves among:

- stakeholders;
- authorities;
- processes;
- services;
- registries;
- decisions;
- controls; and
- reporting mechanisms.

### 35.25 Information Dependency

A governance activity shall identify material information dependencies where failure or staleness could affect the outcome.

### 35.26 Governance Dependency

A governance capability may depend on:

- another capability;
- authority;
- registry;
- information source;
- external service;
- technical platform;
- stakeholder;
- decision; or
- lifecycle state.

Material dependencies shall be identified.

### 35.27 Dependency Criticality

Dependencies may be classified according to:

- criticality;
- substitutability;
- recovery time;
- authority;
- impact; and
- concentration.

### 35.28 Governance Bottleneck

A governance bottleneck occurs where limited capacity, authority, information, or process throughput materially constrains governance outcomes.

Bottlenecks should be monitored and addressed.

### 35.29 Governance Single Point of Failure

A governance single point of failure exists where the loss of one authority, role, service, information source, or process prevents a material governance function from operating.

Material single points of failure should have continuity controls.

### 35.30 Governance Capacity

The operating model shall consider capacity across:

- decision-making;
- assessment;
- review;
- assurance;
- administration;
- information management;
- stakeholder engagement; and
- escalation.

### 35.31 Capacity vs Authority

Having capacity to perform an activity does not create authority to perform it.

### 35.32 Governance Service Levels

Where governance services are operationally significant, service expectations may define:

- response time;
- processing time;
- availability;
- escalation;
- quality;
- evidence requirements; and
- communication.

Service levels shall not override substantive authority or normative requirements.

### 35.33 Governance Queue

Governance processes may maintain controlled queues for:

- decisions;
- approvals;
- assessments;
- changes;
- exceptions;
- disputes;
- publications; or
- other governance work.

Queue state should be visible to authorized participants.

### 35.34 Governance Prioritization

Governance work may be prioritized using:

- materiality;
- risk;
- urgency;
- dependency;
- strategic importance;
- legal or regulatory obligation where applicable; and
- continuity impact.

Priority shall not bypass authority requirements.

### 35.35 Governance Intake

Governance intake provides a controlled mechanism for receiving:

- requests;
- proposals;
- issues;
- changes;
- exceptions;
- disputes;
- assessments; and
- other governance matters.

### 35.36 Intake Validation

Material intake should validate:

- identity;
- scope;
- authority;
- completeness;
- evidence;
- classification;
- urgency; and
- required route.

### 35.37 Governance Routing

A governance matter shall be routed to the appropriate:

- authority;
- process;
- forum;
- service;
- registry; or
- escalation path.

Routing shall not determine the substantive outcome.

### 35.38 Governance State Synchronization

Where a matter crosses multiple governance systems, material state should remain synchronized.

Conflicting state shall be reconciled through the applicable authority.

### 35.39 Cross-Domain Governance Flow

Cross-domain matters shall identify:

- participating domains;
- lead authority;
- affected authorities;
- dependencies;
- decision rights;
- escalation;
- communication; and
- resolution.

### 35.40 Governance Coordination

Coordination mechanisms may include:

- joint reviews;
- shared decision records;
- cross-domain councils;
- dependency reviews;
- synchronized lifecycle events; or
- controlled information exchange.

### 35.41 Governance Handoff

A handoff occurs when responsibility for a governance matter moves between roles, processes, services, or authorities.

Handoffs shall preserve:

- identity;
- state;
- evidence;
- responsibility;
- authority;
- pending actions; and
- deadlines.

### 35.42 Handoff Failure

Handoff failure includes:

- lost responsibility;
- duplicated action;
- missing evidence;
- authority ambiguity;
- stale state; or
- missed deadline.

Material handoff failures shall be treated as governance issues.

### 35.43 Governance Exception Path

Operating models shall provide defined exception paths for cases where normal workflow cannot be followed.

Exception paths shall remain bounded by authority.

### 35.44 Governance Emergency Path

Emergency governance paths may shorten normal workflow while preserving:

- authority;
- scope;
- evidence;
- traceability;
- duration; and
- post-event review.

### 35.45 Governance Recovery Flow

Recovery shall restore not only technical capability but also:

- governance state;
- decision state;
- authority;
- lifecycle;
- evidence;
- queues;
- pending actions; and
- stakeholder communication.

### 35.46 Governance Feedback Loop

The operating model shall support feedback from:

- outcomes;
- monitoring;
- assurance;
- incidents;
- disputes;
- stakeholders;
- audits; and
- performance evaluation

back into planning and governance improvement.

### 35.47 Continuous Feedback

Feedback shall be evaluated for:

- significance;
- recurrence;
- systemic impact;
- required action;
- owner; and
- closure.

### 35.48 Governance Learning

Governance should convert material experience into:

- lessons;
- control improvements;
- process improvements;
- capability improvements;
- normative changes; or
- other authorized governance action.

### 35.49 Operating Model Change

Changes to the governance operating model shall follow applicable change governance.

Material changes shall assess impact on:

- authority;
- roles;
- processes;
- services;
- information;
- dependencies;
- stakeholders;
- continuity; and
- performance.

### 35.50 Operating Model Documentation

The operating model should be represented through controlled artifacts such as:

- capability maps;
- process maps;
- role matrices;
- authority matrices;
- service catalogs;
- dependency maps;
- workflow definitions;
- governance calendars;
- escalation maps; and
- operating procedures.

### 35.51 Operating Model Source of Truth

Where multiple representations exist, the authoritative source for each operating-model attribute shall be identifiable.

### 35.52 Operating Model Versioning

Material operating-model changes shall be versioned and historically reconstructable.

### 35.53 Operating Model Conformance

Governance implementation should be assessed against the applicable operating model where conformance is required.

### 35.54 Operating Model Effectiveness

Operating-model effectiveness should consider:

- decision quality;
- timeliness;
- authority clarity;
- throughput;
- control effectiveness;
- stakeholder experience;
- resilience;
- dependency management; and
- governance outcomes.

### 35.55 Operating Model Metrics

Governance may monitor:

- decision latency;
- approval latency;
- queue age;
- escalation volume;
- handoff failure;
- workflow exceptions;
- service performance;
- bottlenecks;
- capacity utilization;
- unresolved dependencies; and
- control failures.

Metrics are management information unless explicitly made normative.

### 35.56 Operating Model Anti-Patterns

Governance shall guard against:

- authority hidden inside workflow implementation;
- automated decisions without authorized decision rights;
- process ownership confused with normative ownership;
- uncontrolled manual workarounds;
- duplicate governance paths;
- unowned handoffs;
- queues without accountable owners;
- escalation without authority;
- services becoming de facto policy makers; and
- technical systems becoming accidental sources of normative authority.

### 35.57 Operating Model Status

This section establishes the initial Universal Governance Operating Model.

It remains subject to validation against Universal Architecture, the authority model, governance capability model, lifecycle framework, information architecture, continuity model, and final governance operating arrangements.

It is **canonically locked as part of Universal Governance v1.0**.


---

## 36. Universal Governance Capability and Maturity Framework

### 36.1 Purpose

This framework establishes how Universal Governance identifies, defines, assesses, develops, maintains, and improves governance capabilities and their maturity.

Its purpose is to distinguish:

- authority from capability;
- capability from process;
- conformance from maturity;
- activity from effectiveness;
- capacity from capability; and
- maturity assessment from normative approval.

### 36.2 Governance Capability

A Governance Capability is the sustained ability of Universal Governance, a governance domain, role, service, or organizational component to perform a defined governance function within authorized scope.

A capability may be supported by:

- people;
- roles;
- authority;
- processes;
- information;
- technology;
- controls;
- resources;
- knowledge; and
- governance services.

### 36.3 Capability Boundary

Each material capability should define:

- purpose;
- scope;
- owner;
- consumers;
- inputs;
- outputs;
- authorities;
- processes;
- dependencies;
- controls;
- evidence;
- performance indicators; and
- maturity characteristics.

### 36.4 Capability vs Authority

Authority establishes the right to act or decide.

Capability establishes the ability to perform the function.

Possessing capability does not create authority.

### 36.5 Capability vs Process

A capability represents an enduring ability.

A process represents a repeatable method of execution.

One capability may be implemented through multiple processes.

### 36.6 Capability vs Capacity

Capacity is the available quantity or throughput of resources.

Capability is the ability to perform the function reliably.

High capacity does not necessarily indicate mature capability.

### 36.7 Capability vs Conformance

Conformance determines whether applicable requirements are satisfied.

Capability maturity determines how developed and reliable the underlying governance ability is.

A capability may conform while remaining immature.

### 36.8 Capability Portfolio

Universal Governance should maintain a controlled capability portfolio representing material governance capabilities.

The portfolio may identify:

- capability identity;
- purpose;
- owner;
- scope;
- maturity;
- dependencies;
- performance;
- risks;
- strategic importance; and
- lifecycle.

### 36.9 Capability Identity

Material capabilities shall have stable identity sufficient to support:

- ownership;
- assessment;
- dependency mapping;
- planning;
- reporting;
- change; and
- historical reconstruction.

### 36.10 Capability Ownership

Each material capability should have an accountable owner responsible for:

- capability health;
- maturity assessment;
- development;
- dependencies;
- performance;
- risks;
- evidence; and
- improvement.

Ownership does not confer additional normative authority unless explicitly assigned.

### 36.11 Capability Consumers

Capability consumers may include:

- governance authorities;
- domains;
- services;
- processes;
- stakeholders;
- implementations; or
- other governance capabilities.

Consumer needs should inform capability design and improvement.

### 36.12 Capability Dependencies

Capabilities may depend on:

- other capabilities;
- authorities;
- roles;
- information;
- registries;
- technology;
- external services;
- resources; or
- stakeholders.

Material dependencies should be mapped.

### 36.13 Capability Dependency Criticality

Dependencies should be assessed for:

- criticality;
- concentration;
- substitutability;
- failure impact;
- recovery;
- ownership; and
- resilience.

### 36.14 Capability Composition

A capability may consist of:

- sub-capabilities;
- processes;
- services;
- controls;
- roles;
- information assets; and
- enabling resources.

Composition shall preserve the parent capability's semantic boundary.

### 36.15 Capability Decomposition

Decomposition should be used where a capability is too broad to:

- own;
- assess;
- measure;
- improve; or
- govern effectively.

Decomposition shall avoid unnecessary fragmentation.

### 36.16 Capability Enablement

Capabilities may be enabled by:

- policy;
- standards;
- authority;
- people;
- training;
- technology;
- information;
- controls;
- funding;
- infrastructure; or
- partnerships.

### 36.17 Capability Lifecycle

A capability may progress through states such as:

- identified;
- defined;
- designed;
- established;
- operational;
- improved;
- suspended;
- transformed;
- retired; and
- archived.

The capability lifecycle shall remain distinct from the lifecycle of individual governance objects.

### 36.18 Capability Establishment

A new capability should have:

- defined purpose;
- authority basis;
- owner;
- scope;
- consumers;
- operating model;
- required resources;
- controls;
- evidence model; and
- initial performance expectations.

### 36.19 Capability Activation

A capability becomes operational when required conditions for operation are satisfied.

Activation shall not be inferred merely from documentation.

### 36.20 Capability Suspension

A capability may be suspended where:

- integrity is compromised;
- required resources are unavailable;
- authority is suspended;
- critical dependency fails;
- risk becomes unacceptable; or
- another authorized condition exists.

### 36.21 Capability Retirement

Retirement shall address:

- consumers;
- dependencies;
- records;
- outstanding obligations;
- replacement capability;
- transition;
- historical evidence; and
- continuity.

### 36.22 Capability Maturity

Maturity describes the degree to which a capability is:

- defined;
- repeatable;
- controlled;
- measurable;
- integrated;
- resilient; and
- continuously improved.

### 36.23 Maturity Model

Universal Governance may use a multi-level maturity model.

A generic model may include:

1. **Initial** — capability exists inconsistently or depends heavily on individuals.
2. **Defined** — purpose, scope, roles, and core methods are documented.
3. **Managed** — performance, controls, evidence, and ownership are actively managed.
4. **Integrated** — capability is integrated with dependencies, governance flows, and related capabilities.
5. **Optimized** — capability is continuously improved using performance, intelligence, risk, and outcome information.

These levels are a conceptual baseline and shall not be treated as final canonical scoring criteria until formally approved.

### 36.24 Maturity Dimension

Maturity may be assessed across dimensions such as:

- governance;
- process;
- people;
- information;
- technology;
- controls;
- performance;
- integration;
- resilience; and
- improvement.

### 36.25 Maturity Evidence

Maturity assessments shall rely on evidence appropriate to the claimed level.

Evidence may include:

- approved artifacts;
- operating records;
- decisions;
- metrics;
- assessments;
- audit results;
- demonstrations;
- historical performance;
- dependency evidence; and
- improvement records.

### 36.26 Evidence Sufficiency

The evidence required for maturity assessment shall be proportionate to:

- capability materiality;
- claimed maturity;
- risk;
- assessment purpose; and
- assurance level.

### 36.27 Maturity Assessment

A maturity assessment evaluates the current capability against defined maturity characteristics.

It shall identify:

- scope;
- assessment criteria;
- evidence;
- current level;
- limitations;
- gaps;
- confidence; and
- assessor.

### 36.28 Self-Assessment

A capability owner may perform self-assessment where permitted.

Self-assessment shall distinguish:

- claimed maturity;
- independently validated maturity; and
- unresolved evidence gaps.

### 36.29 Independent Assessment

Independent assessment may be required for:

- high-materiality capabilities;
- foundational capabilities;
- disputed maturity;
- major transformation;
- assurance;
- risk-sensitive areas; or
- another defined condition.

### 36.30 Assessment Independence

Assessors should be sufficiently independent from the capability owner to provide credible assessment.

### 36.31 Maturity Score

A maturity score may be expressed as:

- level;
- dimension score;
- qualitative rating;
- confidence rating; or
- another approved representation.

A numerical score shall not be treated as authoritative unless the scoring model is formally established.

### 36.32 Maturity Confidence

Assessment results should include confidence where evidence is incomplete, indirect, stale, or contested.

### 36.33 Maturity Gap

A maturity gap is the difference between:

- current maturity; and
- required or target maturity.

The target must be justified by:

- risk;
- strategy;
- materiality;
- dependency;
- service expectation;
- regulatory requirement where applicable; or
- another authorized basis.

### 36.34 Target Maturity

Not every capability requires the highest maturity level.

Target maturity should be proportionate to:

- purpose;
- criticality;
- risk;
- cost;
- strategic importance;
- dependency;
- expected change; and
- continuity requirements.

### 36.35 Capability Maturity Roadmap

Material maturity gaps should be addressed through a roadmap containing:

- target;
- initiatives;
- dependencies;
- resources;
- owner;
- milestones;
- evidence;
- risks; and
- expected outcomes.

### 36.36 Capability Improvement

Improvement may address:

- process;
- authority;
- role clarity;
- automation;
- controls;
- evidence;
- skills;
- information;
- integration;
- resilience; or
- performance.

### 36.37 Continuous Improvement

Continuous improvement should use:

- performance data;
- assessment;
- audit findings;
- incidents;
- stakeholder feedback;
- lessons learned;
- technology changes;
- risk intelligence; and
- strategic changes.

### 36.38 Improvement Prioritization

Capability improvements may be prioritized by:

- risk reduction;
- outcome impact;
- strategic importance;
- dependency;
- maturity gap;
- effort;
- urgency; and
- continuity.

### 36.39 Capability Risk

Capability risk may arise from:

- insufficient maturity;
- excessive dependency;
- concentration;
- inadequate resources;
- authority ambiguity;
- weak controls;
- obsolete methods;
- knowledge loss; or
- resilience weakness.

### 36.40 Capability Health

Capability health should consider:

- maturity;
- performance;
- risk;
- capacity;
- dependency;
- control effectiveness;
- resilience;
- stakeholder experience; and
- improvement trajectory.

### 36.41 Capability Performance

Capability performance measures whether the capability is producing intended outputs and outcomes.

Performance measures may include:

- quality;
- timeliness;
- reliability;
- throughput;
- accuracy;
- control effectiveness;
- outcome achievement; and
- stakeholder experience.

### 36.42 Capability Effectiveness

Effectiveness evaluates whether capability performance achieves intended governance outcomes.

A capability may perform efficiently without being effective.

### 36.43 Capability Efficiency

Efficiency considers the relationship between:

- resources consumed; and
- governance outputs or outcomes produced.

Efficiency shall not override required controls or authority.

### 36.44 Capability Resilience

Capability resilience is the ability to continue or recover governance function under disruption.

Resilience should address:

- critical dependencies;
- alternate resources;
- succession;
- recovery;
- degraded operation; and
- restoration.

### 36.45 Capability Scalability

A mature capability should be assessed for its ability to operate as:

- volume grows;
- domains increase;
- complexity increases;
- dependencies expand; or
- governance requirements change.

### 36.46 Capability Interoperability

Where capabilities interact, interoperability should preserve:

- identity;
- authority;
- semantics;
- state;
- evidence;
- interfaces; and
- responsibility.

### 36.47 Capability Standardization

Common capability patterns may be standardized where this improves:

- consistency;
- interoperability;
- efficiency;
- control;
- assurance; or
- scalability.

Standardization shall not eliminate legitimate domain variation.

### 36.48 Capability Variation

A capability may vary by domain where:

- variation is authorized;
- scope is explicit;
- core Universal constraints are preserved; and
- differences are traceable.

### 36.49 Capability Federation

Federated capabilities should define:

- shared expectations;
- local responsibility;
- common interfaces;
- evidence;
- maturity comparability;
- escalation; and
- synchronization.

### 36.50 Capability Benchmarking

Capabilities may be benchmarked against:

- prior state;
- peer domains;
- target state;
- approved reference model; or
- external benchmark where appropriate.

Benchmarking shall not itself establish normative requirements.

### 36.51 Capability Assessment Cadence

Assessment frequency should be proportionate to:

- materiality;
- risk;
- change rate;
- maturity;
- strategic importance; and
- external dependency.

### 36.52 Triggered Assessment

A capability should be reassessed when:

- material change occurs;
- significant incident occurs;
- major dependency changes;
- performance degrades;
- risk changes materially;
- target maturity changes; or
- assurance requires reassessment.

### 36.53 Maturity Regression

Maturity may decrease where:

- controls degrade;
- knowledge is lost;
- resources decline;
- dependencies fail;
- performance deteriorates;
- processes become obsolete; or
- governance changes invalidate previous capability.

Maturity shall not be assumed permanent.

### 36.54 Maturity Restoration

Restoration of maturity should require evidence that the underlying capability has recovered, not merely that documentation was updated.

### 36.55 Maturity Transfer

When capability responsibility moves between entities, maturity evidence shall be preserved and reassessed where material.

### 36.56 Capability Dependency Failure

Failure of a critical dependency may temporarily reduce capability maturity or performance.

The resulting state shall be assessed rather than concealed.

### 36.57 Capability Portfolio Balance

The capability portfolio should be assessed for:

- concentration;
- duplicated capability;
- missing capability;
- underutilized capability;
- excessive fragmentation; and
- strategic gaps.

### 36.58 Capability Rationalization

Where capabilities overlap unnecessarily, governance may consolidate, redesign, or retire them.

Rationalization shall preserve required outcomes and authority.

### 36.59 Capability Investment

Investment decisions should consider:

- target maturity;
- risk;
- strategic value;
- performance;
- dependency;
- cost;
- sustainability; and
- expected benefit.

### 36.60 Capability Deinvestment

A capability may be reduced or retired where:

- demand falls;
- risk changes;
- replacement exists;
- strategy changes;
- cost is disproportionate; or
- capability is no longer required.

### 36.61 Capability Transition

Transition to a new capability shall define:

- predecessor;
- successor;
- overlap period;
- migration;
- dependency changes;
- evidence;
- effective state; and
- retirement.

### 36.62 Capability Conformance

Where a capability is subject to normative requirements, conformance shall be assessed independently from maturity where practical.

### 36.63 Capability Assurance

Material maturity claims may require assurance through:

- independent assessment;
- audit;
- evidence review;
- testing;
- peer review; or
- another authorized mechanism.

### 36.64 Capability Reporting

Capability reporting should provide:

- current maturity;
- confidence;
- performance;
- risk;
- target;
- gap;
- roadmap;
- dependencies; and
- trend.

### 36.65 Capability Dashboard

A capability dashboard may aggregate information for authorized governance users.

Dashboards are representations and shall not become authoritative merely through presentation.

### 36.66 Capability Historical State

Governance should support reconstruction of capability maturity and health at a prior time.

Historical maturity records should preserve:

- criteria;
- evidence;
- assessment;
- confidence;
- assessor;
- target; and
- applicable version of the maturity model.

### 36.67 Maturity Model Versioning

Changes to maturity criteria shall be versioned.

A capability assessed under one maturity model version shall not be silently compared with another model version where semantic equivalence is not established.

### 36.68 Maturity Model Transition

When the maturity model changes, governance shall define:

- mapping;
- migration;
- comparability;
- re-assessment;
- historical treatment; and
- effective date.

### 36.69 Capability and Operating Model

Capabilities shall be mapped to the Governance Operating Model.

The mapping should identify:

- processes;
- services;
- roles;
- authorities;
- information;
- controls;
- dependencies; and
- outputs.

### 36.70 Capability and Strategy

Capability priorities should derive from:

- Universal objectives;
- governance strategy;
- risk;
- stakeholder needs;
- architectural direction; and
- required outcomes.

### 36.71 Capability and Resource Planning

Material capability gaps should inform:

- workforce planning;
- technology planning;
- funding;
- training;
- infrastructure;
- succession; and
- service capacity.

### 36.72 Capability and Change

Material capability changes shall use applicable change governance.

Change impact should consider:

- authority;
- dependencies;
- performance;
- maturity;
- continuity;
- consumers; and
- evidence.

### 36.73 Capability and Knowledge

Capability sustainability depends on preservation of:

- institutional knowledge;
- methods;
- rationale;
- evidence;
- training;
- role knowledge; and
- historical experience.

### 36.74 Capability and Succession

Critical capabilities should have succession and continuity arrangements for:

- key roles;
- expertise;
- decision authority;
- operational knowledge; and
- critical dependencies.

### 36.75 Capability and Stakeholders

Capability design and improvement should consider:

- consumer needs;
- stakeholder impact;
- accessibility;
- participation;
- service quality; and
- feedback.

### 36.76 Capability and Risk

Capability maturity should inform risk assessment.

Material capability weakness may be treated as a governance risk even where no current non-conformance exists.

### 36.77 Capability and Performance Improvement

Performance degradation should trigger capability analysis where the issue may be systemic rather than isolated.

### 36.78 Capability Anti-Gaming

Governance shall guard against:

- inflating maturity scores;
- confusing documentation with capability;
- claiming maturity from policy existence alone;
- using compliance as proof of maturity;
- changing criteria to improve scores without authorization;
- excluding unfavorable evidence;
- measuring only easy-to-improve indicators;
- treating capability ownership as proof of capability; and
- declaring maturity without operational evidence.

### 36.79 Capability and Maturity Status

This section establishes the initial Universal Governance Capability and Maturity Framework.

It remains subject to validation against the Governance Operating Model, Performance and Effectiveness Framework, Monitoring and Intelligence Framework, assessment methodology, strategic planning, resource governance, and final Universal maturity model.

It is **canonically locked as part of Universal Governance v1.0**.


---

## 37. Universal Governance Monitoring and Intelligence Framework

### 37.1 Purpose

This framework establishes how Universal Governance observes, measures, correlates, analyzes, interprets, and communicates governance conditions and signals.

Its purpose is to provide an integrated governance intelligence capability that supports:

- timely awareness;
- evidence-based decisions;
- risk detection;
- performance management;
- capability management;
- lifecycle awareness;
- cross-domain coordination;
- early warning; and
- continuous improvement.

### 37.2 Governance Monitoring

Governance Monitoring is the systematic observation and evaluation of governance states, activities, events, conditions, indicators, and outcomes.

Monitoring shall operate within authorized scope and shall not itself create normative authority.

### 37.3 Governance Intelligence

Governance Intelligence is the structured transformation of governance information and signals into insights useful for governance decisions, risk management, performance evaluation, and improvement.

### 37.4 Monitoring vs Intelligence

Monitoring answers:

> **What is happening or what has changed?**

Intelligence answers:

> **What may this mean, why may it matter, and what governance response may be warranted?**

Neither automatically creates a governance decision.

### 37.5 Governance Observability

Governance Observability is the ability to determine the condition and behavior of governance mechanisms from available evidence and signals.

Observability should support:

- state visibility;
- event visibility;
- dependency visibility;
- authority visibility;
- performance visibility;
- risk visibility; and
- historical reconstruction.

### 37.6 Monitoring Scope

Monitoring may cover:

- governance objects;
- lifecycle;
- decisions;
- changes;
- authorities;
- roles;
- capabilities;
- processes;
- services;
- controls;
- risks;
- conformance;
- stakeholders;
- information;
- dependencies;
- resources; and
- outcomes.

### 37.7 Monitoring Objectives

Each material monitoring activity should define:

- objective;
- scope;
- subject;
- indicator;
- source;
- frequency;
- threshold;
- responsible role;
- interpretation;
- escalation; and
- retention.

### 37.8 Monitoring Source

Monitoring sources may include:

- registries;
- decision records;
- lifecycle events;
- audit results;
- conformance assessments;
- operational systems;
- stakeholder feedback;
- incident records;
- performance measurements;
- capability assessments;
- resource data; and
- external information where authorized.

### 37.9 Source Authority

Monitoring shall distinguish authoritative sources from:

- derived data;
- analytical representations;
- estimates;
- external observations;
- user reports; and
- unverified signals.

### 37.10 Signal

A Governance Signal is an observed data point, event, change, pattern, anomaly, or indication relevant to governance.

Signals may be:

- quantitative;
- qualitative;
- categorical;
- temporal;
- relational; or
- composite.

### 37.11 Signal Provenance

Material signals should preserve:

- source;
- observation time;
- collection time;
- transformation;
- scope;
- confidence; and
- applicable version or state.

### 37.12 Signal Reliability

Signals should be evaluated for:

- accuracy;
- completeness;
- freshness;
- consistency;
- provenance;
- bias;
- uncertainty; and
- corroboration.

### 37.13 Indicator

A Governance Indicator is a defined measure used to represent a governance condition, trend, performance characteristic, risk signal, or other governance property.

### 37.14 Indicator Classes

Indicators may include:

- state indicators;
- activity indicators;
- performance indicators;
- outcome indicators;
- risk indicators;
- control indicators;
- capability indicators;
- maturity indicators;
- dependency indicators;
- quality indicators; and
- stakeholder indicators.

### 37.15 Leading Indicator

A leading indicator provides an early signal of a possible future condition.

### 37.16 Lagging Indicator

A lagging indicator reflects a condition or outcome that has already occurred.

### 37.17 Threshold

A threshold defines a condition that may trigger:

- attention;
- investigation;
- escalation;
- review;
- control action; or
- governance decision.

Thresholds shall be interpreted within their defined context.

### 37.18 Threshold Types

Thresholds may be:

- absolute;
- relative;
- statistical;
- temporal;
- trend-based;
- composite;
- risk-based; or
- condition-based.

### 37.19 Alert

An alert is a controlled notification generated when a defined monitoring condition is met.

An alert is not itself a governance decision.

### 37.20 Alert Severity

Alert severity may reflect:

- impact;
- urgency;
- confidence;
- scope;
- persistence;
- reversibility; and
- dependency.

### 37.21 Alert Ownership

Material alerts shall have an accountable owner for:

- triage;
- investigation;
- escalation;
- closure; and
- evidence.

### 37.22 Alert Fatigue

Governance monitoring shall avoid excessive low-value alerts that reduce the ability to identify material conditions.

### 37.23 Event

A Governance Event is a material occurrence that changes or may affect governance state, authority, lifecycle, risk, performance, dependency, or another governed condition.

### 37.24 Event Detection

Material events should be detected through controlled sources.

Detection mechanisms may include:

- registry changes;
- workflow events;
- audit findings;
- threshold breaches;
- stakeholder reports;
- incident systems; or
- other authorized signals.

### 37.25 Event Correlation

Related events may be correlated to identify:

- causal relationships;
- repeated patterns;
- dependency effects;
- systemic conditions;
- escalation;
- emerging risk; or
- coordinated change.

Correlation shall distinguish evidence from inference.

### 37.26 Pattern

A Governance Pattern is a recurring or significant relationship among signals, events, states, or outcomes.

Patterns may indicate:

- improvement;
- degradation;
- concentration;
- recurring failure;
- emerging risk;
- process weakness; or
- structural change.

### 37.27 Trend

A trend represents directional change over time.

Trend interpretation shall account for:

- measurement changes;
- scope changes;
- seasonality where relevant;
- model changes;
- missing data; and
- external factors.

### 37.28 Anomaly

An anomaly is a material deviation from an expected pattern, baseline, threshold, or historical behavior.

An anomaly is a signal for investigation, not automatic proof of failure.

### 37.29 Baseline

A monitoring baseline defines the reference condition against which change may be evaluated.

Baselines shall have:

- scope;
- time period;
- version;
- method;
- assumptions; and
- authority where material.

### 37.30 Governance State View

A Governance State View is a controlled representation of the current or historical condition of a governance subject.

Views may include:

- authority state;
- lifecycle state;
- conformance state;
- risk state;
- capability state;
- performance state;
- dependency state; and
- publication state.

### 37.31 State Aggregation

Multiple lower-level states may be aggregated into a higher-level governance view.

Aggregation rules shall be explicit and shall not conceal material uncertainty.

### 37.32 State Confidence

Aggregated governance states should indicate confidence where:

- evidence is incomplete;
- sources conflict;
- data is stale;
- inference is required; or
- measurement quality is limited.

### 37.33 Governance Dashboard

A Governance Dashboard is a controlled presentation of selected governance information.

A dashboard is a representation and shall not become authoritative merely through presentation.

### 37.34 Dashboard Audience

Dashboards should be tailored to:

- executives;
- governance authorities;
- domain owners;
- capability owners;
- assurance functions;
- operational teams; or
- other authorized consumers.

### 37.35 Dashboard Source Integrity

Material dashboard values should be traceable to authoritative or identified analytical sources.

### 37.36 Governance Intelligence Model

A governance intelligence model may relate:

**Objects → Events → States → Indicators → Patterns → Risks → Insights → Decisions → Outcomes**

The model shall preserve distinctions between observation, analysis, inference, and decision.

### 37.37 Analytical Layer

The analytical layer may perform:

- aggregation;
- comparison;
- trend analysis;
- correlation;
- anomaly detection;
- forecasting;
- scenario analysis;
- dependency analysis; and
- risk analysis.

Analytical outputs shall be distinguishable from authoritative facts.

### 37.38 Inference

An inference is a conclusion derived from available evidence and analysis.

Inference shall not be represented as directly observed fact.

### 37.39 Confidence

Material intelligence outputs should indicate confidence where uncertainty is significant.

Confidence may consider:

- evidence quality;
- source reliability;
- model reliability;
- corroboration;
- data completeness; and
- temporal validity.

### 37.40 Forecast

A forecast estimates a future condition based on available evidence and assumptions.

Forecasts shall identify uncertainty and shall not be represented as certain future states.

### 37.41 Scenario

Scenario analysis evaluates possible future conditions under defined assumptions.

Scenarios support decision-making but do not determine future outcomes.

### 37.42 Early Warning

An early-warning mechanism identifies signals that may precede material governance deterioration or opportunity.

Early warning should provide:

- trigger;
- evidence;
- confidence;
- potential impact;
- recommended investigation;
- owner; and
- escalation.

### 37.43 Risk Signal

A risk signal is an observation indicating a possible increase, decrease, or change in governance risk.

Risk signals shall be linked to the applicable risk framework where material.

### 37.44 Control Signal

A control signal indicates whether a control is:

- operating;
- failing;
- degraded;
- bypassed;
- overdue;
- ineffective; or
- otherwise requiring attention.

### 37.45 Capability Signal

Capability signals may indicate:

- maturity;
- performance;
- capacity;
- dependency;
- resilience;
- degradation; or
- improvement.

### 37.46 Lifecycle Signal

Lifecycle signals may indicate:

- transition;
- expiration;
- suspension;
- supersession;
- withdrawal;
- retirement;
- pending transition; or
- lifecycle conflict.

### 37.47 Authority Signal

Authority signals may indicate:

- role vacancy;
- delegation expiration;
- conflicting authority;
- unauthorized action;
- authority concentration;
- suspension; or
- succession.

### 37.48 Decision Signal

Decision signals may indicate:

- backlog;
- decision latency;
- repeated rejection;
- unresolved conditions;
- conflict;
- appeal;
- emergency decision volume; or
- implementation deviation.

### 37.49 Stakeholder Signal

Stakeholder signals may indicate:

- unresolved concerns;
- participation gaps;
- repeated feedback;
- communication failure;
- representation gaps;
- accessibility issues; or
- stakeholder impact.

### 37.50 Information Quality Signal

Information quality signals may indicate:

- stale records;
- missing metadata;
- broken references;
- conflicting sources;
- incomplete evidence;
- unauthorized changes; or
- provenance gaps.

### 37.51 Dependency Signal

Dependency signals may indicate:

- concentration;
- failure;
- degradation;
- unavailable dependency;
- unresolved ownership;
- synchronization failure; or
- cascading impact.

### 37.52 Resource Signal

Resource signals may indicate:

- capacity shortage;
- critical skill gap;
- budget constraint;
- infrastructure limitation;
- excessive workload;
- succession risk; or
- unsustainable dependency.

### 37.53 Cross-Domain Intelligence

Universal Governance should support aggregation of signals across domains where authorized.

Cross-domain analysis may identify:

- systemic risk;
- shared dependency;
- inconsistent governance;
- duplicated capability;
- conflicting decisions;
- cascading effects; or
- common failure patterns.

### 37.54 Domain Isolation

Cross-domain intelligence shall not expose protected information beyond authorized scope.

### 37.55 Intelligence Escalation

Material intelligence findings should be routed to the appropriate:

- capability owner;
- risk owner;
- decision authority;
- assurance function;
- domain authority; or
- higher governance authority.

### 37.56 Intelligence-to-Decision Boundary

Intelligence informs decisions.

Intelligence systems shall not silently convert analytical findings into normative decisions.

### 37.57 Automated Intelligence

Automated analytics may generate:

- alerts;
- classifications;
- correlations;
- predictions;
- recommendations; or
- prioritization.

Material governance decisions remain subject to authorized decision mechanisms.

### 37.58 Model Governance

Where analytical or predictive models materially influence governance, the model should have:

- owner;
- purpose;
- scope;
- version;
- assumptions;
- training or source data where applicable;
- validation;
- limitations;
- confidence;
- review; and
- retirement.

### 37.59 Model Change
